The KSBA moduli space of stable log Calabi–Yau surfaces
Valery Alexeev, Hülya Argüz, Pierrick Bousseau

Abstract
. We prove that every irreducible component of the coarse Kollár-Shepherd-Barron and Alexeev (KSBA) moduli space of stable log Calabi–Yau surfaces is a finite quotient of a projective toric variety. This verifies a conjecture of Hacking–Keel–Yu. The proof combines tools from log smooth deformation theory, the minimal model program, punctured log Gromov–Witten theory and mirror symmetry.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Context and overview

Constructing moduli spaces along with functorial compactifications, admitting geometrically and combinatorially desirable properties, has been of great interest in algebraic geometry. The fundamental work of Deligne–Mumford
[
28
]
and Knudsen
[
65
]
describes a compactification of the moduli space of smooth genus
g
curves with marked points, using stable curves admitting at worst nodal singularities. More general compactifications, using curves with weighted marked points, were introduced
by Losev–Manin
[
74
]
in the genus
g=0
case, and by Hassett
[
52
]
in higher genus.
One of the striking features of the compactifications of
[
74
]
, known as Losev–Manin spaces, is that they are toric varieties. In this paper, we investigate the geometry of moduli spaces of higher dimensional analogues of “stable curves with weighted marked points”, and show that for stable log Calabi–Yau surfaces such moduli spaces admit finite covers by toric varieties.

The higher dimensional analogue of a stable curve, introduced by Kollár–Shepherd-Barron
[
68
]
and Alexeev
[
5
]
, is a stable pair
(Y,B)
, consisting of a projective variety
Y
and a
\mathbb{Q}
-divisor
B
such that
(Y,B)
has semi-log-canonical (slc) singularities, and satisfying the
stability condition
requiring
K_{Y}+B
to be
\mathbb{Q}
-Cartier and ample – see
[
66
]
for a comprehensive treatment of the KSBA theory.
The geometry of the KSBA moduli space of stable pairs has been investigated so far in a handful of cases, including toric and abelian varieties
[
6
]
, K3 surfaces
[
7
,
8
,
10
,
72
]
, elliptic surfaces
[
20
,
21
]
, and for some surfaces of general type
[
9
]
.
Most relevant to the present paper,
Alexeev showed in
[
6
]
that
for toric varieties the normalization of the coarse
KSBA moduli space is toric, with associated fan the secondary fan defined as in
[
37
]
.

More generally, for
maximal log Calabi–Yau varieties
, it has been conjectured by Keel
[
63
]
around 2012 that the coarse KSBA moduli space shall still admit a finite cover by a toric variety. Furthermore, Hacking–Keel–Yu conjectured that such a toric variety should be defined by a generalized version of the “secondary fan” coming from mirror symmetry
[
50
]
. We prove this conjecture in dimension two, that is, for all polarized
maximal log Calabi–Yau surfaces

(Y,D,L)
consisting of a projective surface
Y
over
\mathbb{C}
, with a reduced, anticanonical divisor
D\subset Y
, such that
(Y,D)
has at worst log canonical singularities, and an ample line bundle
L
on
Y
. Furthermore,
(Y,D)
is required to be
maximal
, in the sense that
D
is non-empty and singular.

The KSBA moduli space for
(Y,D,L)
, denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
, is the closure in the moduli space of stable pairs of the locus of stable pairs deformation equivalent to
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
, where
C\in|L|
and
0<\epsilon<\!\!<1
. It is a proper Deligne–Mumford stack, with a projective coarse moduli space
M_{(Y,D,L)}
.
To investigate the geometry of
M_{(Y,D,L)}
via mirror symmetry, we construct a “semistable mirror” to a maximal degeneration of
(Y,D,L)
. Such a degeneration is determined by a choice of a polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
on a “Symington polytope”
P
associated to
(Y,D,L)
. To obtain the semistable mirror, we first build a normal crossing surface with dual intersection complex
(P,\mathscr{P})
. The semistable mirror is then obtained as a formal smoothing of this normal crossing surface. We show that such a smoothing is an
open Kulikov degeneration

\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\to\Delta=\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}\llbracket t\rrbracket
, given by a projective crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}
of a formal affine canonical 3-fold singularity

\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
We then define the “secondary fan”
\mathrm{Sec}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}/\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}})
, as a coarsening of the Mori fan for the semistable mirror to
(Y,D,L)
. Our main result, Theorem
8.11
, states:

Theorem A.

The coarse KSBA moduli space
M_{(Y,D,L)}
for a polarized toric log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
admits a finite cover

S_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}^{\mathrm{sec}}\longrightarrow M_{(Y,D,L)}\,,

where
S_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}^{\mathrm{sec}}
is the toric variety associated to the secondary fan
\mathrm{Sec}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}/\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}})
.

We provide an outline of the key results involved in the proof in what follows, after briefly explaining why it goes via mirror symmetry.

1.2.
Motivation from mirror symmetry

Given a stable log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
with an ample line bundle
L
, let
U:=Y\setminus D
, and
s
be the section of
L|_{U}
defining
C|_{U}
. One can naturally associate to
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
the non-compact Calabi–Yau 3-fold
Z
given by the hypersurface with equation
xy=s
in the total space of the rank two vector bundle
L|_{U}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{U}
over
U
, where
x
and
y
are linear coordinates along the fibers. Said differently,
Z
is the affine conic bundle over the non-compact Calabi–Yau surface
U
branched over the curve
C|_{U}
. Heuristically, the KSBA moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
can be viewed as a compactification of a “moduli space of complex structures” on
Z
. Mirror symmetry predicts an identification of
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
with the “stringy extended Kähler moduli space” of a mirror Calabi–Yau 3-fold
\mathcal{X}
to
Z

[
77
]
. More precisely, under mirror symmetry different maximal degenerations of
Z
should correspond to different mirrors related by birational transformations. In this paper, working directly with
(Y,D,L)
instead of
Z
, we provide a precise realization of this heuristic picture,
where a mathematically rigorous definition of the “stringy extended Kähler moduli space of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}
” is given by the toric variety
S_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}^{\mathrm{sec}}
.

It is worth noting that in the situation when
Y
is a toric variety, and
D
is the toric boundary divisor, mirror symmetry is entirely combinatorial and it essentially reduces to a duality between fans and momentum polytopes.
Let
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
be the singular toric variety with associated fan
C(P)
, the cone over the momentum polytope
P
of
(Y,D,L)
. Choosing a maximal regular subdivision
\mathscr{P}
and refining
C(P)
by adding cones over
\mathscr{P}
, one obtains a toric crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, the semistable mirror family to
(Y,D,L)
. By standard toric geometry, the Mori fan of this mirror family to
(Y,D,L)
coincides with the secondary fan of
(Y,D,L)

[
25
]
. So, the KSBA moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
for a polarized toric log Calabi–Yau surface can be understood combinatorially, either in terms of the secondary fan, or of the Mori fan of the mirror
[
6
,
98
]
.
The existence of a dense torus in
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
follows in this case from the existence of the basis of
H^{0}(Y,L)
given by the monomials
z^{m}
on
(\mathbb{C}^{\star})^{2}=Y\setminus D
indexed by the integral points
m\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}:=P\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. The key feature of this basis is that, for a curve
C
defined by an equation
\sum_{m\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}}a_{m}z^{m}=0
with
a_{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
for all
m\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the pair
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
is KSBA-stable. Indeed, since for any vertex
m^{\prime}
of
P
, having
a_{m^{\prime}}\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
ensures that
C
does not intersect the singular point of
D
corresponding to
m^{\prime}
, it follows that
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
has only slc singularities if
a_{m}\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
for all
m\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}

[
6
]
.

Obtaining a toric description of the KSBA moduli space for general non-toric log Calabi–Yau surfaces, in particular finding a dense torus first inside the KSBA moduli space, again amounts to finding a basis for
H^{0}(Y,L)
, having the analogous key feature as the monomial basis in the toric case. However, by contrast to the toric case, purely combinatorial tools no more suffice to do so. Nonetheless, we show that such a basis is given by “theta functions”, defined by (a version of) the Gross–Siebert intrinsic mirror symmetry construction
[
48
,
49
]
, applied to the semistable mirror to
(Y,D,L)
. In restriction to a torus
(\mathbb{C}^{\star})^{2}\subset Y\setminus D
, the theta functions are particular Laurent polynomials with positive integer coefficients. However, no elementary combinatorial description of these Laurent polynomials is known. To describe them, following
[
48
,
49
]
, we use enumerative geometry, more precisely, particular counts of rational curves in the semistable mirror, defined using the theory of punctured Gromov–Witten invariants of Abramovich–Chen–Gross–Siebert
[
2
]
.

1.3.
Outline of the paper

In §
2
, we investigate maximal degenerations of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces combinatorially, using real affine geometry. To do so, we consider a “nice” toric model of a polarized log Calabi–Yau
(Y,D,L)
, as in previous work of Engel–Friedman
[
31
]
, which allows us to systematically describe an associated “Symington polytope”
P
to
(Y,D,L)
given by an integral affine manifold with singularities
[
91
]
.
We define the notion of a
good
polyhedral decomposition on
P
,
and we prove the existence of such decompositions in Theorem
2.14
.
A good polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
on
P
defines in particular a maximal degeneration of
(Y,D,L)
, whose central fiber has “intersection complex”
(P,\mathscr{P})
, as explained in §
2.4
.

To construct a “mirror” to such a maximal degeneration of
(Y,D,L)
, we first construct a normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
with “dual intersection complex”
(P,\mathscr{P})
in §
4.2
.
The irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
are obtained by
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein smoothings of toric surfaces with quotient Wahl singularities, using Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2
which show that there are no local to global obstructions for such smoothings. Finally, we appropriately glue these irreducible components to obtain the normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, which has features similar to the central fiber of a Type III Kulikov degeneration in the context of K3 surfaces
[
35
]
. A key difference in our context is that the dual intersection complex of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is a disk, rather than a sphere. So, in §
3
we introduce the general notion of an
open Kulikov surface
, as a normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
with dual intersection complex a disk, and satisfying some additional properties – see Definition
3.7
. After investigating properties of such surfaces, as well as of their affinizations, we show in §
4
that for an appropriate gluing the surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
associated is a
d
-semistable open Kulikov surface. Our key results in Theorems
3.26
and
3.29
prove the following result for a general open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
:

Theorem B.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be a quasi-projective open Kulikov surface.
Then, there exists a quasi-projective formal smoothing
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that the total space is smooth and
\mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathcal{X}
is a reduced normal crossing divisor, that is
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a semistable degeneration. Moreover,
K_{\mathcal{X}}=0
and the affinization morphism
\mathcal{X}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is a projective crepant resolution.

We refer to such a smoothing
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
as an
open Kulikov degeneration
. By the Minimal Model Program, adapted to the formal set up
[
75
]
, we conclude in §
3.6
that such a Kulikov degeneration is a relative Mori dream space. Hence, we obtain a complete toric fan, the “MoriFan”, in
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})\otimes\mathbb{R}
. Then, following
[
50
]
, we define the “secondary fan”
\mathrm{Sec}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
as a “coarsening” of this Mori fan, by identifying neighboring cones connected by “M1 flops” – for details see §
6.4
, and denote by
S_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}^{\mathrm{sec}}
the corresponding toric variety.

For a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
with a choice of
(P,\mathscr{P})
, we refer to the open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
obtained by smoothing
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
as the “semistable mirror” of
(Y,D,L)
.
We then investigate the “double mirror”, obtained by applying a version of the intrinsic mirror construction of Gross–Siebert
[
48
]
to the semistable mirror
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\to\Delta
.
More generally, we construct an intrinsic mirror for any quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
.
One subtlety in our setup is that
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is only quasi-projective, while the intrinsic mirror construction of
[
48
]
applies to projective degenerations. Therefore, we first prove in Theorem
5.2
that the moduli spaces of punctured log Gromov–Witten invariants of an open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, defining the structure constants in the intrinsic mirror construction, are proper. The proof crucially uses the fact that we have a projective crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is affine.
Hence, analogously as in
[
48
]
, we define a commutative and associative graded mirror algebra
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
– see Theorem
5.5
. Thus, taking the Proj of this algebra, we obtain an intrinsic mirror family to
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
a priori defined over the formal scheme
\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}\llbracket NE(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})\rrbracket
, as in
[
48
]
, where
NE(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
stands for the monoid of integral points in the cone of relative effective curve classes of
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
.

One of our key results, Theorem
6.16
, shows that, for any quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
, only finitely many curve classes of punctured log curves contribute to each structure constant of the intrinsic mirror algebra. This shows:

Theorem C.

The intrinsic mirror family to a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
naturally extends to a family
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{X}}\to S_{\mathcal{X}}
over the affine toric variety
S_{\mathcal{X}}:=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[NE(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})]
, with fan the nef cone
\mathrm{Nef}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
.

The proof of this uses the birational geometry of the crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
to constrain the curve classes appearing in the intrinsic mirror construction.

By construction
[
48
]
, the intrinsic mirror algebra
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{X}}
admits a basis of
theta functions

\{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in C(P)_{\mathbb{Z}}}
, indexed by the integral points of the tropicalization of
\mathcal{X}
given by the cone
C(P)
over
P
. It follows by Lemma
6.22
that the theta functions
\vartheta_{p}
corresponding to the interior integral points of
C(P)
define an ideal of the mirror algebra
R_{\mathcal{X}}
. We prove that this ideal defines an anticanonical divisor
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{X}}
in
\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
On the other hand, the intrinsic mirror family is naturally polarized by the line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{X}}}(1)
with a natural basis of sections
\vartheta_{p}
, for
p\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
and so we have a natural “theta divisor”
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}}:=\{\sum_{p\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}}\vartheta_{p}=0\}\in|\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{X}}|
.
Hence, running the intrinsic mirror construction for every projective crepant resolution
\mathcal{X}^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
of the affinization
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
of
\mathcal{X}
, we obtain pairs
\left(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}},D_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}+\epsilon\,\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{X}^{\prime}}\right)
over the affine toric varieties
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[NE(\mathcal{X}^{\prime}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})]
.
In §
6
we show that these families glue to a family over the movable secondary fan and in Theorem
6.28
we show that this family extends uniquely to a family
(\mathcal{Y}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{sec}},\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}}+\epsilon\,\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}})\to\mathcal{S}^{sec}_{\mathcal{X}}
, where
\mathcal{S}^{sec}_{\mathcal{X}}
is a toric Deligne-Mumford stack defined by a stacky fan
\mathcal{S}ec(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})

with underlying fan the secondary fan
\mathrm{Sec}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
.
Theorems
7.13
and
8.10
then prove:

Theorem D.

The intrinsic mirror family

\left(\mathcal{Y},D+\epsilon\,\mathcal{C}\right)
to a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
is a family of KSBA stable log Calabi–Yau surfaces over the toric stack
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}}
.
Moreover,
when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}
is the semistable mirror of a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
, the general fiber of this intrinsic mirror family is deformation equivalent to
(Y,D,L)
.

To prove that the fibers
(Y_{t},D_{t}+\epsilon\,C_{t})
of the intrinsic mirror family are slc, we first show in Theorem
7.7
that over the dense torus of the
toric stack
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{sec}}
, the surface
Y_{t}
is irreducible, and then it is enough to
show that
C_{t}
does not pass through the
0
-strata of
D_{t}
. This follows from the description in §
6.4.2
of the restriction of the theta functions to
D_{t}
.
For an arbitrary fiber
(Y_{t},D_{t}+\epsilon\,C_{t})
, with irreducible components
(V_{i},D_{i}+\epsilon\,C_{i})
, we show using Theorem
7.12
that it suffices to prove that the pairs
(V_{i},D_{i}+\epsilon\,C_{i})
are slc. By the naturalness
of the intrinsic mirror symmetry, this follows from Theorem
7.7
.

To study the general fiber of the intrinsic mirror family to the semistable mirror
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
, we show that, up to a base change of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
, a particular one-parameter sub-family of the intrinsic mirror family is a toric degeneration in the sense of Gross–Siebert
[
47
]
. This allows us to identify the general fiber topologically, and then as a log Calabi–Yau surface using a result of Friedman
[
34
]
. Then, by the universal property of the moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
, we obtain a map of stacks
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{(Y,D,L)}
, inducing a map

S^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow M_{(Y,D,L)}
at the level of coarse moduli spaces.
To conclude our main result, in Theorem
8.11
we show that this map is finite and surjective. The finiteness follows by proving that the intrinsic mirror family is non-constant in restriction to the one-dimensional strata of
\mathcal{S}^{\mathrm{sec}}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}}
, which suffices by
[
50
, Lemma 8.7]
.
Finally, the surjectivity is proved using a period calculation, as in
[
71
,
83
]
.

1.4.
Related work

Mirrors to
log smooth
maximal degenerations to (log) Calabi–Yau varieties are constructed by Gross–Siebert in
[
48
]
. As the maximal degenerations of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces we consider in this paper are typically not log smooth, the construction of
[
48
]
does not apply directly to produce the semistable mirror
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
. Also, the mirror construction of Gross–Hacking–Keel
[
41
]
concerns a single “smooth” log Calabi–Yau surface, without maximal degenerations, and hence it does not apply either.
To address this issue, we give a direct construction by deformation theory of the semistable mirror
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
.
If we restrict attention to smooth log Calabi–Yau surfaces, we expect the affinization
\mathcal{\overline{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}
of our semistable mirror
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}
to be deformation equivalent to the mirror of
[
41
]
. However, the existence of a crepant resolution in that setup is not immediate.
Moreover, in order to eventually relate the geometry of the “double mirror” to the original polarized log Calabi–Yau surface, the semistable mirror construction introduced in our paper plays a crucial role.

To construct the double mirror to
(Y,D,L)
, that is, the intrinsic mirror to
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
, we use a version of the Gross–Siebert mirror construction
[
48
,
49
]
, which is possible since
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
is log smooth. In
[
50
]
, the non-Archimedean mirror construction developed by Keel–Yu
[
64
]
is used instead to verify the Hacking–Keel–Yu conjecture for
6
stable log Calabi–Yau surfaces
(Y,D+\epsilon\,C)
, namely when
Y
is a del Pezzo surfaces of degree
n
, for
1\leq n\leq 6
, endowed with the line bundle
L=-K_{Y}
, and
D
a cycle of
n

(-1)
-curves. These surfaces are in a sense “self-mirror”, for example, the dimension of the complex moduli equals the dimension of the Kähler moduli, and due to this coincidence one can conveniently take the mirror to any of these del Pezzo’s
Y
as the canonical bundle
K_{Y}
. In this paper, to prove the Hacking–Keel–Yu conjecture for all log Calabi–Yau surfaces, we investigate double mirrors by studying intrinsic mirrors to our semistable mirrors, applying (a version of) the construction of
[
48
]
rather than the non-Archimedean mirror construction of
[
64
]
. To be able to use the non-Archimedean mirror construction in this generality, it would first be necessary to have an extension of the results of
[
64
]
over a field with nontrivial valuation, which is not yet available
1
1

1

After the second author presented results of this paper at the Homological Mirror Symmetry conference on January 28th 2024 at IMSA/Miami, we were informed that Logan White is currently working on such an extension in his PhD under the guidance of Sean Keel.
.

Finally, the ongoing work of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Siebert
[
44
]
on describing compactifications of moduli spaces of K3 surfaces using intrinsic mirror symmetry is also closely related, as open Kulikov degenerations introduced in our paper are non-compact analogues of the Type III Kulikov degenerations of K3 surfaces – see also
[
54
]
and
[
55
]
in this context.

1.5.
Acknowledgments

2.
Polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces and Symington polytopes

In this section, we first review in §
2.1
the definition and basic properties of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces.
Then, we explain in §
2.2
, following
[
31
]
, how to associate to such surfaces “nice” choices of “Symington polytopes”.
The main result of this section is the existence of “good” polyhedral decompositions on such polytopes, obtained in Theorem
2.14
in §
2.3
. Finally, we describe in §
2.4
how to construct maximal degenerations of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces from good polyhedral decompositions on Symington polytopes.

2.1.
Log Calabi–Yau surfaces

In this paper, a
log Calabi–Yau surface
is a log canonical (lc) pair
(Y,D)
over
\mathbb{C}
, as in
[
67
, Definition 2.34]
, with
Y
projective of dimension
2
,
D
a reduced effective divisor, and
K_{Y}+D
trivial, in particular Cartier. Throughout this paper we assume that all log Calabi–Yau surfaces are
maximal
, in the sense that
D
is non-empty and singular. The following proposition classifies singularities of log Calabi–Yau surfaces.

Proposition 2.1
.

Let
(Y,D)
be a log Calabi–Yau surface. Then, away from the divisor
D
, the surface
Y
has at worst du Val (ADE) singularities, and the germ of
(Y,D)
at any point
p\in D
is given by either of the following:

i)

(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{x,y},(x=0))
: both
Y
and
D
are smooth locally around
p
.

ii)

(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{x,y},(xy=0))
:
Y
is smooth and
D
has an ordinary double point locally around
p
.

iii)

(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{x,y}/\frac{1}{r}(1,a),(xy=0))
, where
r
and
a
are coprime integers, with
r\geq 2
and
1\leq a\leq r-1
:
Y
has a cyclic quotient singularity at
p
, obtained as the quotient of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{x,y}
by
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
acting by
x\to\zeta x
,
y\to\zeta^{a}y
, where
\zeta
is a primitive r’th root of
1
. Moreover, locally around
p
,
D
is the image under the quotient by
\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
of the union of the coordinate axes in
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{x,y}
, and consequently
D
has an ordinary double point at
p
.

Proof.

See
[
50
, Propositions 7.2–7.3]
.
∎

Note that while
(Y,D)
is in general singular, we can often work with its minimal resolution.

Corollary 2.2
.

Let
g^{\prime}:Y^{\prime}\to Y
be the minimal resolution of
Y
, and
D^{\prime}=(g^{\prime})^{-1}(D)
be the pre-image of
D
. Then,
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
is a smooth log Calabi–Yau surface.

Proof.

The statement follows from Proposition
2.1
and the toric description of minimal resolutions of cyclic quotient singularities
[
25
, Chapter 10]
.
∎

Example 2.3
.

Let
\overline{Y}
be a projective toric surface, and
\overline{D}
the union of the toric divisors of
\overline{Y}
. Then,
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
is a log Calabi–Yau surface.

We will consider polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces
(Y,D,L)
, given by a log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D)
endowed with an ample line bundle
L
. In what follows we describe toric models for such surfaces. For this, we first define first define interior and corner blow-ups.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
(Y_{1},D_{1})
and
(Y_{2},D_{2})
be two log Calabi–Yau surfaces. We say that
g:Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
is a
corner blow-up
if
g
is a birational morphism whose exceptional locus is a union of irreducible components of
D_{1}
, and

D_{1}=g^{-1}(D_{2})
.

Definition 2.5
.

Let
(Y_{1},D_{1})
and
(Y_{2},D_{2})
be two log Calabi–Yau surfaces. We say that
h:Y_{1}\rightarrow Y_{2}
is an
interior blow-up
if
h
is the blow-up of
Y_{2}
at a point on the smooth locus of
D_{2}
, and
D_{1}
is the strict transform of
D_{2}
by
h
.

We can now define the notion of a toric model of a polarized log Calabi–Yau pair.

Definition 2.6
.

A
toric model
of a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
is a diagram of log Calabi–Yau surfaces

(2.1)

(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D})
(Y,D)
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})

g

h

where
g
is a corner blow-up,
h
is a composition of interior blow-ups,
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
is a toric log Calabi–Yau surface, and
\overline{L}:=h_{\star}g^{\star}L
is an ample line bundle on
\overline{Y}
.

Every Calabi-Yau surface admits a toric model by
[
41
, Lemma 1.3]
applied to the minimal resolution given by Corollary
2.2
.
We will use particularly good toric models, defined as follows.

Definition 2.7
.

A
good toric model
of a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
is a toric model as in Definition
2.6
such that the following conditions hold:

(i)

h
is a composition of interior blow-ups at distinct points of
\overline{D}
.

(ii)

There exists a decomposition

g^{\star}L=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\,h^{\star}\overline{D}_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}m_{ij}E_{ij}\,,

of
g^{\star}L
in terms of the irreducible components
(\overline{D}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
\overline{D}
, and of the exceptional divisors
(E_{ij})_{1\leq j\leq N_{i}}
of
h
above
\overline{D}_{i}
, such that we have

c_{i}\geq 0
,
m_{ij}\geq 0
, and
m_{ij}\leq c_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Every polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
admits a good toric model.

Proof.

This follows from the proof of
[
31
, Theorem 5.4]
applied to the smooth log Calabi–Yau pair
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
obtained as the minimal resolution
g^{\prime}:(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})\rightarrow(Y,D)
as in
Corollary
2.2
, and to the big and nef line bundle

L^{\prime}:=(g^{\prime})^{\star}L
. Note that, while
[
31
, Theorem 5.4]
is stated for an ample line bundle, its proof applies more generally to a big and nef line bundle as remarked in
[
31
, Remark 5.7]
.
∎

The following result is well-known for smooth log Calabi–Yau surfaces and is included for reference in the singular case.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
(Y,D)
be a log Calabi–Yau surface. Then, the Picard group
\mathrm{Pic}(Y)
is torsion-free.

Proof.

By the existence of a toric model, it follows that
Y
is isomorphic to a projective toric surface
\overline{Y}
, up to loci of complex dimension one, that is, of real dimension two. Hence, we have
H_{1}(Y,\mathbb{Z})=H_{1}(\overline{Y},\mathbb{Z})=0
, so
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
is torsion free, and
\mathrm{Pic}(Y)
is torsion free by the exponential exact sequence.
∎

2.2.
Symington polytopes

Associated to a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
, there is a so called Symington “polytope”
P
, given by an integral affine manifold with singularities, which corresponds to the base of an almost toric fibration
Y\to P

[
91
, §
2.2
]
. Engel and Friedman showed in
[
31
]
that one can make nice choices of Symington polytopes, so that they are obtained from a toric momentum polytope by cutting out non-overlapping triangles with one edge adjacent to the boundary. In this section we review this construction, following
[
31
, §
5.1
]
.

Construction 2.10
.

We describe the construction of a Symington polytope for a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
endowed with a good toric model
(\overline{Y},\overline{D},\overline{L})
as in Definition
2.7
.
We denote by
\overline{P}
the toric momentum polytope associated to the ample line bundle
\overline{L}
on the toric surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
.
The edges
(\overline{P}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
of the boundary
\partial\overline{P}
of
\overline{P}
are naturally in one-to-one correspondence with the toric irreducible components
(\overline{D}_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
\overline{D}
. Moreover, for every
1\leq i\leq n
, the integral length
\ell(\overline{P}_{i})
of the edge
\overline{P}_{i}
is given by the intersection number
\overline{L}\cdot\overline{D}_{i}
.

As in Definition
2.7
, we denote the exceptional curves of the blow-up
h:\widetilde{Y}\to\overline{Y}
contracted to
\overline{D}_{i}
by
E_{ij}
, for
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
.
By Definition
2.7
,
there exists a decomposition

(2.2)

\widetilde{L}:=g^{\star}L=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\,h^{\star}\overline{D}_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}m_{ij}E_{ij}\,,

such that

c_{i}\geq 0
,
m_{ij}\geq 0
, and
m_{ij}\leq c_{i}

for all
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
.
Moreover, we have

(2.3)

\sum_{j}m_{ij}\leq\ell(\overline{P}_{i})

where
\ell(\overline{P}_{i})
is the lattice length of
\overline{P}_{i}
, since
g^{*}L
is nef and hence

g^{*}L\cdot\tilde{D}_{i}=\overline{L}\cdot\overline{D}_{i}-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}m_{ij}=\ell(\overline{P}_{i})-\sum_{j=1}^{N_{i}}m_{ij}\geq 0\,,

using that
\overline{L}=h_{\star}g^{\star}L=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\overline{D}_{i}
.

As
\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\overline{D}_{i}
is an effective torus-invariant divisor on
\overline{Y}
representing the line bundle
\overline{L}
, there exists a corresponding integral point

p\in\overline{P}
at lattice distance
c_{i}
from each edge
\overline{P}_{i}
of
\overline{P}
.
The momentum polytope
\overline{P}
admits a polyhedral decomposition,

\overline{P}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\overline{T}_{i}\,,

where
\overline{T}_{i}
is the convex hull of
\overline{P}_{i}
and the point
p
. Generally
\overline{T}_{i}
’s are triangles, unless
p\in\partial\overline{P}
, in which case they may degenerate into line segments.

Due to Equation (
2.3
), we can choose non-overlapping line segments
\overline{P}_{ij}\subset\overline{P}_{i}
of lattice length
m_{ij}
.
We denote by
\overline{T}_{ij}
the triangle with base
\overline{P}_{ij}
and third vertex
p
, and by
\overline{L}_{ij}\subset\overline{T}_{ij}
the line segment of points in
\overline{T}_{ij}
at lattice distance
m_{ij}
from
\overline{P}_{i}
. Note that
\overline{L}_{ij}
is non-empty since we have
m_{ij}\leq c_{i}
by definition of a good toric model.

By the proof of
[
31
, Proposition 5.8]
, one can find an integral point
p_{ij}
on
\overline{L}_{ij}
.
Let
\Delta_{ij}
be the triangle with base
\overline{P}_{ij}
and third vertex
p_{ij}
.
By construction,
\Delta_{ij}
is an integral triangle, with base
\overline{P}_{ij}
equal to
m_{ij}
, and with integral height also equal to
m_{ij}
. We refer to
m_{ij}
as the size of
\Delta_{ij}
. We then define the Symington polytope
P
associated to
(Y,D,L)
as the integral affine manifold with singularities
P
obtained from complement
\overline{P}\setminus\cup_{ij}\mathrm{Int}(\Delta_{ij})
, by gluing the edges adjacent to
p_{ij}
using a matrix conjugate to

\left(\begin{matrix}1&1\\
0&1\end{matrix}\right)\,.

This amounts to introducing a particular integral affine singularity, known as a focus-focus singularity, at each point
p_{ij}
. The corresponding monodromy invariant direction is the line
L_{ij}
parallel to the edge
\overline{P}_{i}
and passing through
p_{ij}
.
The discriminant locus of the affine structure is given by the union of points

\Delta=\{p_{ij}~|~1\leq i\leq n~\mathrm{and}~1\leq j\leq N_{i}\}\,.

Figure 2.1.
A Symington polytope for a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
as in Construction
2.10
.

We refer to the vertex
p_{ij}
of
\Delta_{ij}
as the
interior vertex
of
\Delta_{ij}
, and to the edges of
\Delta_{ij}
adjacent to
p_{ij}
as the
interior edges
of
\Delta_{ij}
. Note that, sometimes the interior vertices of several triangles
\Delta_{ij}
may coincide, leading to more complex singularities on
Y
than focus-focus singularities.

The following result shows that the monodromy invariant lines have always a non-trivial intersection with the Symington polytope
P
.

Lemma 2.11
.

For every
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
, the intersection of the monodromy invariant line
L_{ij}
with the interior of
P
is an interval of positive length.

Proof.

Denote by
\ell_{ij}=0
the affine equation of
\overline{P}_{i}
. Then
L_{ij}
is the line of equation
\ell_{ij}=m_{ij}
. Denote by
d_{ij}
the greatest integral distance between the line
L_{ij}
and a point
p\in P\cap\{\ell_{ij}\geq m_{ij}\}
. To prove Lemma
2.11
, it is enough to show that
d_{ij}>0
.

We will prove that
d_{ij}>0
by giving a geometric interpretation of
d_{ij}
.
Let
(\overline{Y}^{\prime},\overline{D}^{\prime})
be the toric variety obtained from
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
by adding to its fan the ray opposite to the ray corresponding to
\overline{P}_{i}
.
These two opposite rays in the fan define a generically
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\overline{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Denote by
(\tilde{Y}^{\prime},\tilde{D}^{\prime})
the corresponding blow-up of
(Y,D)
, with a generically
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration
\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Then, the pre-image of
E_{ij}
is an irreducible component
E_{ij}^{\prime}
of a fiber of this map. Denote by
F_{ij}
the other irreducible component of this fiber and
g^{\prime}:\widetilde{Y}^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
the contraction induced by
g
. Then, we have
d_{ij}=L\cdot g^{\prime}_{\star}F_{ij}
. Since
g^{\prime}
is a corner blow-up, we have
g^{\prime}_{\star}F_{ij}\neq 0
, and so we conclude that
d_{ij}=L\cdot g^{\prime}_{\star}F_{ij}>0
since
L
is ample.
∎

2.3.
Good polyhedral decompositions

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface with associated Symington polytope
P
, obtained from a toric polytope
\overline{P}
as in Construction
2.10
. In this section, we first define in Definition
2.13
the notion of a good polyhedral decomposition on
\overline{P}
, which is a particular regular polyhedral decomposition appropriately compatible with the cuts producing the Symington polytope. Then, we prove the existence of such good polyhedral decompositions in Theorem
2.14
.

An integral polyhedral decomposition of
\overline{P}
is given by a covering
\overline{\mathscr{P}}=\{\sigma\}
of
\overline{P}
by a finite number of strongly convex polyhedra satisfying the following:

i)

If
\sigma\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\subset\sigma
is a face, then
\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
.

ii)

If
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
, then
\sigma\cap\sigma^{\prime}
is a common face of
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
.

iii)

Each polyhedron
\sigma
has all of its vertices at integral points of
\overline{P}
.

We denote by
\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{[k]}
the set of
k-dimensional cells of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
. A polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
on
\overline{P}
is called
regular
if there exists a convex piecewise linear function
\varphi:\overline{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that the polyhedra
\{\sigma\}
of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
corresponds to the domain of linearity of
\varphi
. In this situation, we refer to
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
as the decomposition induced by
\varphi
. In what follows, we describe good regular polyhedral decompositions on
\overline{P}
. For this we first need the following observation.

Proposition 2.12
.

The number of integral points on the two interior edges of a triangle
\Delta_{ij}
, for
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
are equal.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, assume coordinates for the vertex
p_{ij}
are
(0,0)
, and for the other two vertices of
\Delta_{ij}
on
\overline{P}_{ij}
are
(a,b)
and
(a,c)
respectively, with
b>c
. Since the edge of
\Delta_{ij}
on the side
\overline{P}_{ij}
has the same integral length as the height of
\Delta_{ij}
by Construction
2.10
, we have
a=b-c
. Then, we obtain
d:=gcd(a,b)=gcd(a,c)
, and the sets of integral points on the edges connecting
p_{ij}=(0,0)
to
(a,b)
and to
(a,c)
respectively have the same cardinalities, equal to
d+1
:

\left|\left\{\left(\frac{ka}{d},\frac{kb}{d}\right)\,\ |\,\ 0\leq k\leq d\right\}\right|=\left|\left\{\left(\frac{ka}{d},\frac{kc}{d}\right)\,\ |\,\ 0\leq k\leq d\right\}\right|=d+1.

∎

Now we call the decomposition of
\Delta_{ij}
, into the union of the triangle
\Delta_{ij,0}
whose set of vertices is

(2.4)

\left\{(0,0),\left(\frac{a}{d},\frac{b}{d}\right),\left(\frac{a}{d},\frac{c}{d}\right)\right\}\,,

and the
(d-1)
trapezoids
\Delta_{ij,k}
whose set of vertices are

(2.5)

\left\{\left(\frac{ka}{d},\frac{kb}{d}\right),\left(\frac{(k+1)a}{d},\frac{(k+1)b}{d}\right),\left(\frac{ka}{d},\frac{kc}{d}\right),\left(\frac{(k+1)a}{d},\frac{(k+1)c}{d}\right)\right\},

for
1\leq k\leq d-1
, a
standard decomposition
of
\Delta_{ij}
.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
\overline{P}
be the momentum polytope associated to a toric model
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
of a log Calabi–Yau pair
(Y,D)
with an ample line bundle
L
and
\Delta_{ij}
the triangles that we cut out from
\overline{P}
to obtain the Symington polytope
P
, as explained in the Construction
2.10
. A
good polyhedral decomposition
on
\overline{P}
is a regular integral polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
, satisfying the following:

i)

Each of the triangles
\Delta_{ij}
is a union of some polyhedra
\sigma\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
which form a standard decomposition of
\Delta_{ij}
.

ii)

Each polyhedron
\sigma\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
not contained in a triangle
\Delta_{ij}
, is a triangle of size
1
, that is, a triangle whose all edges have integral length
1
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a log Calabi–Yau pair with an ample line bundle with associated Symington polytope
P
obtained from the momentum polytope
\overline{P}
of a toric model
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
by cutting out triangles
\Delta_{ij}
as in Construction
2.10
. Then, there exists a good polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
on
\overline{P}
.

Proof.

First, start with the polyhedral subdivision
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{0}
of
\overline{P}
given by the union of the triangles
\overline{T}_{i}
.
Let
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{1}
be the polyhedral decomposition obtained by adding to
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{0}
the edges joining
p
to all vertices of the edges of
\Delta_{ij}
’s – this is possible to do without any intersection with the edges of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{0}
since Construction

2.10
ensures that the triangles
\Delta_{ij}
’s lie inside triangles
\overline{T}_{ij}
’s which intersect at most along common edges. Note that the polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{1}
of
\overline{P}
is regular – see Figure
2.1
, and so there exists
a convex PL function

\varphi_{1}:\overline{P}\to\mathbb{R}
inducing
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{1}
.

Recall that, by Proposition
2.12
, there are the same number integral points on each of the two interior edges of
\Delta_{ij}
’s. Let
\{a^{1}_{ij},\ldots,a^{d}_{ij}\}
and
\{b^{1}_{ij},\ldots,b^{d}_{ij}\}
respectively denote the set of integral points except from the interior vertex
p_{ij}
, on the interior edges of
\Delta_{ij}
. Let
{\epsilon_{ij}^{1},\ldots,\epsilon_{ij}^{d}}
be a set of positive real numbers such that
the points

(2.6)

(p_{ij},\varphi_{0}(p_{ij}))\,,(a_{ij}^{1},\varphi_{0}(a_{ij}^{1})-\epsilon_{ij}^{1})\,,...,\,,(a_{ij}^{d},\varphi_{0}(a_{ij}^{d})-\epsilon_{ij}^{d})

are exactly the bending points of a PL function on the interior edge of
\Delta_{ij}
containing the points
a_{ij}^{k}
’s. As
\varphi_{1}
is affine on
\Delta_{ij}
, it follows that the points

(2.7)

(p_{ij},\varphi_{0}(p_{ij}))\,,(b_{ij}^{1},\varphi_{0}(b_{ij}^{1})-\epsilon_{ij}^{1})\,,...,\,,(b_{ij}^{d},\varphi_{0}(b_{ij}^{d})-\epsilon_{ij}^{d})

are also exactly the bending points of a PL function on the interior edge of
\Delta_{ij}
containing the points
b_{ij}^{k}
’s.
Let
\varphi_{2}:\overline{P}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be the convex PL function whose graph is the lower convex hull of
the points in (
2.6
), the points in (
2.7
), and
the points
(v,\varphi(v))
, where
v
are vertices of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{1}

not contained in triangles
\Delta_{ij}
. Denote by
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
the regular decomposition of
\overline{P}
induced by
\varphi_{2}
. If
\epsilon_{ij}^{1},\cdots,\epsilon_{ij}^{d}
are small enough for all
1\leq i\leq n
,
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
, then
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
satisfies condition (i) of Definition
2.13
.

To obtain a good decomposition, that also satisfies condition (ii) of Definition
2.13
, we start with
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
and then successively pull generically all the integral points
v
of
\overline{P}
which are not vertices of

\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
, that is we successively modify the convex function by lowering its value at
v
by a small general enough positive amount – see for instance
[
27
, §4.3]
. The resulting decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
, illustrated in Figure
2.2
, coincides with
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
on each triangle
\Delta_{ij}
, and so also satisfies condition (i) of Definition
2.13
. Moreover, on the complement in
\overline{P}
of the interior of the triangles
\Delta_{ij}
,
\overline{\mathscr{P}}

is a triangulation containing all integral points,
and so
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
satisfies condition (ii) of Definition
2.13
, since by Pick’s formula an integral triangle containing no integral point apart from its vertices is necessarily a triangle of size 1.

∎

Figure 2.2.
The left hand figure illustrates
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{1}
and
\overline{\mathscr{P}}_{2}
. To obtain the final decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
the red edges are inserted, as in the right hand figure.

2.4.
Maximal degenerations

Given a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
with associated Symington polytope
P
obtained from
\overline{P}
as in Construction
2.10
, we have a natural polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
on
P
obtained by restricting the good polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
on
\overline{P}
to
P
. In this section, we describe how to construct a degeneration of
(Y,D,L)
, which is
maximal
, in the sense that the central fiber has a
0
-dimensional stratum. Here, stratum stands for the intersection of irreducible components of the central fiber.

Let
\varphi
be a convex PL function on
\overline{P}
defining the regular polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
.
We will construct a one-parameter maximal polarized degeneration

(\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi},\mathcal{D}_{\varphi},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
, with general fiber deformation to
(Y,D,L)
.

We start with the Mumford toric degeneration

(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi},\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi},\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

of the polarized toric surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{D},\overline{L})
induced by the polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
of
\overline{P}

[
39
, Example 3.6]
.
The intersection complex of the central fiber

(\overline{Y}_{\varphi,0},\overline{D}_{\varphi,0},\overline{L}_{\varphi,0})

is the polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
of
\overline{P}
:
the irreducible components of the central fiber are the polarized toric surfaces
Y^{f}
with momentum polytopes the 2-dimensional faces
f
of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
and they are glued along toric divisors
Y^{e}
corresponding to the edges
e
of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
.

For every
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq N_{i}
, we pick a section
s_{ij}
of

(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi},\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi},\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
whose image in the general fiber is contained in the interior of the irreducible component
\overline{D}_{i}
of
\overline{D}
, and
whose image in the central fiber is a point
x_{ij}
in the interior of the divisor
Y^{\overline{P}_{ij}}
corresponding to the edge
\overline{P}_{ij}
of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
in the boundary of
\overline{P}
, as in
Construction
2.10
.

Let
h_{\varphi}:\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi}
be the blow-up of
\overline{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi}
along the sections
s_{ij}
. We denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi}
the strict transform of
\overline{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi}
, and by
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}
the line bundle on
\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi}
defined by

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}=h_{\mathscr{P}}^{\star}\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}-\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{ij}\mathcal{E}_{ij}\,,

where
\mathcal{E}_{ij}
are the exceptional divisors.
Comparing with Equation (
2.2
), it follows that the general fiber of

(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

is deformation equivalent to
(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D},\widetilde{L})
.
While
\widetilde{L}
is a nef line bundle, the restriction
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi,0}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi}
to the central fiber
(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\varphi,0},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\varphi,0})
is not nef if there exists
i
and
j
with
m_{ij}>1
.
Indeed, if
m_{ij}>1
, then the exceptional divisor
\mathcal{E}_{ij,0}
on the central fiber is obtained by blowing-up a point on the divisor
Y^{\overline{P}_{ij}}
, which is contained in the toric surface

Y^{\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
with momentum polytope given by the trapezoid
\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}}
as in Equation (
2.5
). As
\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}}
is a trapezoid of size
1
, it follows that
X^{\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration whose fiber class
F
satisfies
\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi,0}\cdot F=1
.
After the blow-up, the strict transform of the fiber class passing through the blown-up point is a
(-1)
-curve
C_{ij}
of class
F-\mathcal{E}_{ij,0}
, and so
we have

\widetilde{L}_{\varphi,0}\cdot C_{ij}=1-m_{ij}
,
which is negative if
m_{ij}>1
.

Figure 2.3.
Flop to turn
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\varphi,0}
into a nef line bundle.

In order to obtain a nef line bundle, we first replace the 3-fold
\widetilde{Y}_{\varphi}
by its flop along the
(-1,-1)
-curve
C_{ij}
.
On the special fiber, this means blowing up the component corresponding to
\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}-1}
instead of
\Delta_{ij,m_{ij}-1}
. If
m_{ij}>2
, then the line bundle is not nef either, because the intersection with the strict transform of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fiber containing the blown-up point is
2-m_{ij}
, and one can flop this curve – see Figure
2.3
. In general, after
m_{ij}-1
flops, we obtain a family
(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}_{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{\varphi})\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
,
whose general fiber is still deformation equivalent to
(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D},\widetilde{L})
, but with now the line bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{\varphi}
being relatively big and nef. By
[
34
, Theorem 4.18]
, a big enough power of
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{\varphi}
is base point free, and so defines a relatively birational contraction

g:(\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}^{\prime}_{\varphi},\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}^{\prime}_{\varphi})\rightarrow(\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi},\mathcal{D}_{\varphi})\,,

and a relatively ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}

on
\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi}
such that
\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}_{\varphi}=g^{\star}\mathcal{L}_{\varphi}
. The general fiber of the polarized degeneration

(2.8)

(\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi},\mathcal{D}_{\varphi},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi})\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

is deformation equivalent to
(Y,D,L)
, and the intersection complex of the central fiber
(\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi,0},\mathcal{D}_{\varphi,0},\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,0})
is given by the Symington polytope
P
with its polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
. In particular, since
\mathscr{P}
is a decomposition into triangles of size one, it follows that every irreducible component of
\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi,0}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\varphi,0}
to each irreducible component of
\mathcal{Y}_{\varphi,0}
is the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(1)
.

3.
Open Kulikov surfaces and their semistable smoothings

In this section we introduce open Kulikov surfaces and show that
d
-semistable open Kulikov surfaces admit formal semistable smoothings. We also show that these formal smoothings are Mori dream spaces using results of the Minimal Model Program
[
23
]
adapted to the formal set up as in
[
75
]
.

3.1.
Open Kulikov surfaces

Open Kulikov surfaces are normal crossing surfaces whose irreducible components are particular types of surfaces called
0
-surfaces,
1
-surfaces, and
2
-surfaces, defined as below.

3.1.1.
0
-,
1
-, and
2
-surfaces

Both
1
- and
2
-surfaces will be examples of open log Calabi–Yau surfaces as defined below.

Definition 3.1
.

An
open log Calabi–Yau surface

(Y,D)
is a smooth complex surface
Y
, together with a non-empty reduced normal crossing divisor
D\subset Y
such that there exists a smooth log Calabi–Yau surface
(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D})
and a non-empty connected curve
Z
in
\widetilde{Y}
given by a union of irreducible components of
\tilde{D}
, such that
Y=\widetilde{Y}\setminus Z
and
D=\tilde{D}\setminus Z
.

Let
(Y,D)
be an open log Calabi–Yau surface as in Definition
3.1
. Then, for a compactification
(\widetilde{Y},\widetilde{D})
of
(Y,D)
as in Definition
3.1
, the divisor
\widetilde{D}
has at least two irreducible components, and so is a cycle of projective lines.
It follows that the divisor
D
is a chain
D_{0}+\cdots+D_{n}
, where
D_{0}\simeq D_{n}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and

D_{i}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
for
1\leq i\leq n-1
.

The terminology used in the following definitions is inspired by
[
86
,
87
]
.

Definition 3.2
.

A
0
-surface
is an open log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D)
obtained by interior and corner blow-ups of an affine toric surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
, where the interior blow-ups are all done above the torus fixed point of
\overline{Y}
.

If
(Y,D)
is a
0
-surface, then one can uniquely recover
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
as in Definition
3.2
as the affinization of
(Y,D)
, that is,

(\overline{Y},\overline{D}):=(\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y}),\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(D,\mathcal{O}_{D}))\,,

and the affinization morphism
(Y,D)\rightarrow(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
is projective, surjective, and birational. In particular,
Y
contains only finitely many compact irreducible curves, which are either compact irreducible components of
D
, or irreducible components of generalized exceptional curves. Moreover,
\overline{Y}
is a cyclic quotient singularity,
Y\rightarrow\overline{Y}
is a resolution of singularities, and the union of compact curves in
Y
is the corresponding exceptional divisor.
In particular, we have
C^{2}<0
for every compact curve in
Y
.

Example 3.3
.

The fan of the affine toric surface
(\overline{Y},\overline{D})
is a strictly convex rational cone
\sigma
in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. A toric
0
-surface is then a toric surface whose fan is a refinement of
\sigma
.
Finally, any
0
-surface
(Y,D)
is obtained from a toric
0
-surface
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
by interior blow-ups. The tropicalization of
(Y,D)
is obtained by introducing focus-focus singularities moving along the interior rays of the fan of
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, and pushing them to the origin, as in
[
41
]
.

Definition 3.4
.

A
1
-surface
is an open log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D)
such that there exists a projective surjective morphism

Y\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
, contracting the compact irreducible components of
D
to
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

If
(Y,D)
is a
1
-surface, then one can recover
(\mathbb{A}^{1},0)
as the affinization of
(Y,D)
, that is,

(\mathbb{A}^{1},0):=(\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(Y,\mathcal{O}_{Y}),\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(D,\mathcal{O}_{D}))\,.

Moreover, the natural projection
(Y,D)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{1},0)
is the affinization morphism, and is projective, surjective, and generically a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration. Moreover, every compact irreducible curve of
Y
is contained in a fiber of
(Y,D)\rightarrow(\mathbb{A}^{1},0)
, and so
Y
contains a
1
-dimensional family of compact curves, but no
2
-dimensional family of compact curves.
In particular, we have
C^{2}\leq 0
for every compact curve in
Y
, and we have
C^{2}=0
if and only if
C
is a fiber of the ruling
Y\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Example 3.5
.

A toric
1
-surface is a toric surface whose fan has support a half-plane in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Any
1
-surface
(Y,D)

is obtained from a toric
1
-surface
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
by interior blow-ups and corner blow-downs. The tropicalization of
(Y,D)
is obtained by introducing focus-focus singularities moving along the interior rays of the fan of
(Y^{\prime},D^{\prime})
, pushing them to the origin, as in
[
41
]
, and eventually removing some interior rays.

Definition 3.6
.

A
2
-surface
is a smooth log Calabi–Yau surface.

Note that a
2
-surface is projective, and so is covered by families of compact curves of dimension
\geq 2
.

3.1.2.
Open Kulikov surfaces

Definition 3.7
.

An
open Kulikov surface
is a reduced normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
satisfying the following conditions:

i)

The dual intersection complex
B
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}

is topologically a disk.

ii)

Let
\{X^{i}\}_{i\in I}
be the set of irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and denote by
\{v_{i}\}_{i\in I}
the corresponding vertices in
B
. For every
i\in I
, denote the union of double curves in
X^{i}
by
\partial X^{i}=\cup_{j\in I\setminus\{i\}}X^{i}\cap X^{j}
. Denote by
\tilde{X}^{i}
the normalization of
X^{i}
and by
\partial\tilde{X}^{i}
the pre-image of
\partial X^{i}
in
\tilde{X}^{i}
.
Then, for each interior vertex
v_{i}\in\mathrm{Int}(B)
, the pair
(\tilde{X}^{i},\partial\tilde{X}^{i})
is a 2-surface, and for each boundary vertex
v_{i}\in\partial B
, the pair
(\tilde{X}^{i},\partial\tilde{X}^{i})
is
either a
0
-surface or a
1
-surface.

iii)

The surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is
combinatorially
d
-semistable
, that is, for every irreducible component
D_{ij}=X^{i}\cap X^{j}
, we have

(D_{ij}|_{X^{i}})^{2}+(D_{ij}|_{X_{j}})^{2}=-2+2g(D_{ij})\,,

where
g(D_{ij})
is the arithmetic genus of
D_{ij}
.

Lemma 3.8
.

An open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is Gorenstein and has trivial dualizing line bundle, that is,
\omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
.

Proof.

By Definition
3.7
, the irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
are either open or compact log Calabi–Yau surfaces glued in a normal crossing way along anticanonical divisors, and so the result follows.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

If one replaces i) in Definition
3.7
by the condition that
B
is a sphere, and ii) by the condition that
(\tilde{X}^{i},\partial\tilde{X}^{i})
is a
2
-surface for every vertex
v_{i}
of
B
, then one obtains the definition of a Type III Kulikov surface, appearing in the context of Type III Kulikov degenerations of K3 surfaces
[
7
,
35
,
70
,
81
]
.
On the other hand, if one still replaces i) by the condition that
B
is a sphere, but one rather replaces ii) by the condition that every
(\tilde{X}^{i},\partial\tilde{X}^{i})
is a
2
-surface, except one which is a Hirzebruch-Inoue surface, then one obtains the definition of a “Type III anticanonical pair”, appearing in the context of smoothings of cusp singularities in
[
30
,
31
]
.

The dual intersection complex
B
of an open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a simplicial complex, containing a vertex for every irreducible component, a 1-simplex for each irreducible component of the double locus, and a 2-simplex for each triple point. By Definition
3.7
i), the topological realization
P
of
B
is homeomorphic to a disk, and we denote by
\mathscr{P}
the triangulation into triangles of integral size
1

induced by the simplicial structure.
Moreover, it follows from Definition
3.7
iii) that the natural integral affine structure on the interior of the triangles of
\mathscr{P}
can be naturally extended to the complement of the vertices of
\mathscr{P}
. This integral affine structure extends over a vertex
v_{i}
if the corresponding irreducible component
(X^{i},\partial X^{i})
is a toric pair, but not in general.
We refer to
(P,\mathscr{P})
as the
tropicalization
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, where
P
is viewed as an integral affine disk with singularities.

3.1.3.
Properties of open Kulikov surfaces

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be an open Kulikov surface with tropicalization
(P,\mathscr{P})
.
We denote by
X^{v}
,
X^{e}
, and
X^{f}
the irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, the irreducible components of the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and the triple points of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, indexed by the vertices
v
, the edges
e
, and the 2-dimension faces
f
of
\mathscr{P}

respectively.

Lemma 3.10
.

For every open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, we have

H^{i}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})=0
for
i>0
, and

\displaystyle\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}_{0})=H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathbb{Z})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}}\mathrm{Pic}(X^{v})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathrm{Pic}(X^{e})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}}H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}H^{2}(X^{e},\mathbb{Z})\right)\,.

Proof.

As the irreducible components
X^{v}
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
are log Calabi–Yau surfaces and the irreducible components
X^{e}
of the double locus are projective lines, we have

H^{i}(X^{v},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}})=H^{i}(X^{e},\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}})=0
for
i>0
,
\mathrm{Pic}(X^{v})=H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{Pic}(X^{e})=H^{2}(X^{e},\mathbb{Z})
.
Lemma
3.10
then follows from the resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
given by the complex

0\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}_{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}_{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow 0\,,

the corresponding resolution of

\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}}^{\star}
, and the fact that the tropicalization
P
is topologically a disk and so
H^{0}(P,\mathbb{Z})=0
, and
H^{i}(P,\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i>0
.

∎

3.2.
Affinization of open Kulikov surfaces

In this section, we construct for every open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
a proper surjective morphism
f_{0}\colon\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
to a non-normal affine surface
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.
We show that
f_{0}
is the affinization of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and that the affine surface
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

is a degenerate cusp singularity.

Let

\mathcal{X}_{0}=\bigcup_{v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}}X^{v}

be an open Kulikov surface, with tropicalization
(P,\mathscr{P})
, where we denote by
X^{v}
the irreducible component of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
corresponding to the integral points
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

We denote by
P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
(resp.
P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
and
P_{\mathbb{Z}}(2)
) the set of integral points
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
X^{v}
is a 0-surface (resp. a 1-surface and a 2-surface).
By Definition
3.7
ii), for every boundary integral point
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we have either
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
or
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
.
By Definition
3.7
i),
P
is topologically a disk, and so the boundary
\partial P
is topologically a circle. If one fixes an orientation of
\partial P
, we have a corresponding cyclic orientation of the boundary vertices
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}=P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)\cup P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
.
We denote by
(v_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}
the cyclically ordered vertices in
P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
.
Moreover,
P_{\mathbb{Z}}(2)
is exactly the set of vertices of
\mathscr{P}

contained in the interior of
P
.

For every integral point

v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote by
(\overline{X}^{v},\partial\overline{X}^{v})
the affinization of
(X^{v},\partial X^{v})
, that is,

(\overline{X}^{v},\partial\overline{X}^{v}):=(\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(X^{v},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}),\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(\partial X^{v},\mathcal{O}_{\partial X^{v}}))\,.

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(2)
, then
X^{v}
is compact, and so

(\overline{X}^{v},\partial\overline{X}^{v})
is just a point. If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
, then
(\overline{X}^{v},\partial\overline{X}^{v})=(\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathbb{A}^{1})
by Definition
3.4
. Finally, for
i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
, corresponding to an integral point
v_{i}\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
,
the affinization

(\overline{X}^{v_{i}},\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})
is an affine toric surface by Definition
3.6
. Moreover,

\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}}
is the corresponding toric divisor, that is, the union of two irreducible toric divisors
(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})_{i-1}
and
(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})_{i+1}

corresponding to the two boundary edges of
\mathscr{P}
adjacent to
v_{i}

and contained in the intervals
[v_{i-1},v_{i}]
and
[v_{i},v_{i+1}]
of
\partial P
respectively. Both
(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})_{i-1}
and
(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})_{i+1}
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, with
0
corresponding to the torus fixed point of
\overline{X}^{v_{i}}
.

We define

(3.1)

\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}:=\bigcup_{v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)}\overline{X}^{v}=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}}\overline{X}^{v_{i}}\,,

where for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}/N\mathbb{Z}
, we identify

\overline{X}^{v_{i}}
and
\overline{X}^{v_{i+1}}

along their toric divisors

(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i}})_{i+1}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
and
(\partial\overline{X}^{v_{i+1}})_{i}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
by the isomorphism induced by the gluing isomorphism between the irreducible components

(\partial{X}^{v_{i}})_{i+1}
and
(\partial{X}^{v_{i+1}})_{i}
of the double locus
\mathcal{X}_{0}

corresponding to the two boundary edges of
\mathscr{P}
adjacent to
v_{i}
. In particular, this isomorphism
preserves
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and so identifies the torus fixed points of
\overline{X}^{v_{i}}
and
\overline{X}^{v_{i+1}}
.

By construction, the irreducible components of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
are the the affine toric surfaces
\overline{X}^{v_{i}}
, and so
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

is itself affine by
[
51
, Exercise III.3.2]
. Moreover, the double locus of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a union of copies of affine lines

(\partial X^{v_{i}})_{i+1}\simeq(\partial X^{v_{i+1}})_{i}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and all intersecting at a single common point
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, referred below as the
central point
of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, see Figure
3.1
. It follows that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a
degenerate cusp singularity
as in
[
86
, §1]
, and so is in particular Gorenstein semi-log-canonical by
[
68
, Theorem 4.21]
.

Figure 3.1.
Affinization
f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
of an open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Finally, we construct a morphism

f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

as follows.
We first define a morphism
f^{v}:X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
for every integral point
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}
.
To do that, we distinguish the three cases of
0
-,
1
-, and
2
-surface:

(i)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
,
we define
f^{v}
as the composition of the affinization map

X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}
of the
0
-surface
X^{v}
with the inclusion
\overline{X}^{v}\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
of
\overline{X}^{v}
as an irreducible component of

\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.

(ii)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
,
contained in the interval
[v_{i},v_{i+1}]
in
\partial P
,
we define
f^{v}
as the composition of the affinization map
X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}

of the
1
-surface
X^{v}

with the inclusion

(\partial X^{v_{i}})_{i+1}\simeq(\partial X^{v_{i+1}})_{i}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}\subset\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
of the irreducible component

(\partial X^{v_{i}})_{i+1}\simeq(\partial X^{v_{i+1}})_{i}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
of the double locus of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.

(iii)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(2)
, that is,
v\notin(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we define
f^{v}
as the constant map contracting the compact

2
-surface
X^{v}
to the central point
x_{0}\in\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P},0}
.

By construction, the morphisms
f^{v}
and
f^{v^{\prime}}
agree on the intersections

X^{v}\cap X^{v^{\prime}}
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
,
and so they glue together to produce a morphism
f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The morphism
f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a contraction, that is, a surjective proper morphism with
(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}
.
Moreover, we have
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}=0
for all
i>0
.

Proof.

For every
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the affinization map
X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}
is proper and surjective, and so
f_{0}
is also proper and surjective.
To compute
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
, one uses the resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
given by the complex

0\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}}\rightarrow 0\,,

as in the proof of Lemma
3.10
.
The result for
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
follows from the following vanishing results:

(i)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(0)
, then,
\overline{X}^{v}
is an affine toric surface, so with cyclic quotient, and so rational, singularities, and the affinization map
X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}
is a resolution of singularities. It follows that
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}=0
for
i>0
.

(ii)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(1)
, then the affinization map
X^{v}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}
is generically a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibration, and we have
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}=0
for
i>0
.

(iii)

If
v\in P_{\mathbb{Z}}(2)
is an interior vertex or if
e
is an interior edge of
\mathscr{P}
, then
f_{0}
is constant on
X^{v}
or
X^{e}
, and so
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{v}}=R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{e}}=0
for
i>0
follows from the
vanishings
H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{v},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{v}})=H^{i}(\mathcal{X}^{e},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{e}})=0
, which hold since
\mathcal{X}^{v}
is a rational surface and we have
\mathcal{X}^{e}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

(iv)

If
e
is a boundary edge of
\mathscr{P}
,
then the induced map

X^{e}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{e}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
is an isomorphism. In particular, we have
R^{i}(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}}=0
for
i>0
.

(v)

The tropicalization
P
is topologically a disk, and so we have
H^{0}(P,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}
and
H^{i}(P,\mathbb{Z})=0
for
i>0
.

∎

Lemma 3.12
.

We have
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})
,
and
f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\longrightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

is the affinization morphism of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately since
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is affine, and we have
(f_{0})_{\star}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}=\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}}
by Lemma
3.11
.
∎

3.3.
d
-semistable and generic open Kulikov surfaces

In this section, we first review the notion of
d
-semistable surface, and prove a criterion for the
d
-semistability of an open Kulikov surface.
We then introduce a notion of generic open Kulikov surface, and prove in Theorem
3.21
that every open Kulikov surface admits a locally trivial
d
-semistable and generic deformation.

Recall from
[
34
]

that a normal crossing surface
X
, with singular locus
X_{\mathrm{sing}}
, is called
d
-semistable
if
\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega_{X},\mathcal{O}_{X})\cong{\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{\mathrm{sing}}
.
We will study
d
-semistability of open Kulikov surfaces, similarly as in
[
7
, §7.B]
, where
d
-semistability of Type III Kulikov surfaces is studied.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\bigcup_{i}(V_{i},D_{i})
be an open Kulikov surface, with irreducible components
(V_{i},D_{i})
. Denote by
D_{ij}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}
the irreducible components of the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
As in
[
7
, §7.B]
, fixing toric models of the components
(V_{i},D_{i})
, one can deform
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by either moving the position of the internal blow-ups of the toric models, or by changing the gluing along the irreducible components of the double locus. As in Definition
[
7
, Definition 7.6]
,
one can define a gluing complex
\mathcal{G}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{G}_{0}=\mathbb{Z}^{Q}\oplus\bigoplus_{i<j}\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathcal{G}_{1}=\bigoplus_{i}\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, and
Q
is the total number of internal blow-ups in the toric models, that is, the sum of the charges
Q_{i}
of the log Calabi–Yau surfaces
(V_{i},D_{i})
as in
[
34
, Definition 1.1]
. Then, as in
[
7
, Proposition 7.11]
, one can define a universal family
f_{\mathrm{univ}}:\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\mathrm{univ}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(H^{0}(\mathcal{G}),\mathbb{C}^{\star})
of locally trivial deformations of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, where
H^{0}(\mathcal{G})
is the kernel of the gluing complex.

On the other hand, as in
[
7
, Definition 7.8]
, one can define a period complex
\mathcal{P}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{1}
, where
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\bigoplus_{i}\mathrm{Pic}(V_{i})
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\bigoplus_{i<j}\mathrm{Pic}(D_{ij})
, and we denote by
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
the kernel of the period complex.
By Lemma
3.10
, we actually have
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}=\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}_{0})
. As in
[
7
, Proposition 7.9]
, one can show that the gluing complex and the period complex are quasi-isomorphic, and so we have a natural identification
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\simeq H^{0}(\mathcal{G})
between their kernels, and so the universal family
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\mathrm{univ}}

is in fact defined over
\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
.

Define
\xi_{i}:=\sum_{j}(D_{ij}-D_{ji})\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
. For example, when
\mathcal{X}_{0}
admits a smoothing
\mathcal{X}
,
\xi_{i}
is the class of the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}}(-V_{i})|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
in
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}_{0})=\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
.
In general, for every element
\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
, we define
\psi_{i}:=\psi(\xi_{i})\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
.

Lemma 3.13
.

For every
\psi\in\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
,
the open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}:=f_{\mathrm{univ}}^{-1}(\psi)
is
d
-semistable if and only if, for every
i
with compact
V_{i}
, we have
\psi_{i}=1
.

Proof.

The normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}
is
d
-semistable if
T^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}}=\mathcal{E}xt^{1}(\Omega^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}})\simeq\mathcal{O}_{D}
, where
D
is the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}
, see
[
33
,
35
]
. The sheaf
T^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}}
is an invertible sheaf on
D
and its class lies in
\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(D)=\mathrm{Hom}(H_{1}(\Gamma(D),\mathbb{C}^{*}))\simeq(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{g}
, where
\Gamma(D)
is the dual graph of
D
and
g
is its genus. The group
H_{1}(\Gamma(D))
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{g}
, with a basis consisting of the loops
\Gamma(D_{i})
corresponding to the boundaries of the compact components
V_{i}
. The restriction
T^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}}|_{D_{i}}
is
\psi_{i}\in\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(D_{i})=\mathbb{C}^{*}
. It follows that
T^{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}}=1
in
\operatorname{Pic}^{0}(D)
if and only if all
\psi_{i}=1
.
∎

It follows from Lemma
3.13

that
\mathcal{X}_{0,\psi}
is
d
-semistable if and only if

\psi
is contained in the torus
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})\subset\mathrm{Hom}(\widetilde{\mathbb{L}},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
, where
\mathbb{L}
is the the quotient of
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
by the span of the classes
\xi_{i}

such that
V_{i}
is compact.
The following result shows how
\psi
changes by changing the gluing of the irreducible components.

Lemma 3.14
.

For every
\mathcal{L}\in\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
,
changing the gluing along
D_{ij}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}
by multiplication by
c_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
changes
\psi(\mathcal{L})
by

\psi(\mathcal{L})\mapsto\psi(\mathcal{L})\prod_{i<j}c_{ij}^{\mathcal{L}\cdot D_{ij}}\,.

Proof.

This follows from
[
7
, Theorem 7.9]
by tracing the quasi-isomorphism between the Picard complex of and the gluing complex, which gives an explicit embedding of
\mathbb{L}
into
\mathbb{Z}^{E}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{Q}
, where
E=\{D_{ij}\mid i<j\}
is the set of edges in the dual complex of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and
Q
is the set of internal blowups. Changing the gluings acts on
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{Z}^{E},\mathbb{C}^{*})
in an obvious way, by multiplication by
\prod_{i<j}c_{ij}^{\mathcal{L}\cdot D_{ij}}
.
∎

Lemma 3.15
.

Consider a component
D_{ij}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}
of the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
which is not nodal genus one curve. Let
V_{k}
and
V_{l}
the two irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
such that
D_{ijk}:=D_{ij}\cap V_{k}
and
D_{ijl}:=D_{ij}\cap V_{l}
are the two triple points of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
on
D_{ij}
. Then, changing the gluing along
D_{ij}
by multiplication by
c_{ij}\in\mathbb{C}^{\star}
changes the periods
\psi_{m}
as follows:

\displaystyle\psi_{i}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\psi_{i}\,c_{ij}^{D_{ij}^{2}+2}

\displaystyle\psi_{j}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\psi_{j}\,c_{ij}^{D_{ji}^{2}+2}

\displaystyle\psi_{k}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\psi_{k}\,c_{ij}^{-1}

\displaystyle\psi_{l}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\psi_{l}\,c_{ij}^{-1}

\displaystyle\psi_{m}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\psi_{m}\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,m\neq i,j,k,l\,.

Proof.

The result follows by applying Lemma
3.14
to
\mathcal{L}=\xi_{i}
.
∎

For every
i
, define
\mathbb{L}_{i}
as the kernel of the natural map

\mathrm{Pic}(V_{i})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{j}\mathrm{Pic}(D_{ij})\,.

By
[
34
, Lemma 1.6]
,
arbitrary moving the position of the internal blow-ups of the fixed toric model produces a universal family

(V_{i}^{\mathrm{univ}},V_{i}^{\mathrm{univ}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
, where every log Calabi–Yau pair deformation equivalent to
(Y,D)
appears.
The map
\mathbb{L}_{i}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}
, obtained by composition of the natural map
\mathbb{L}_{i}\rightarrow\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}
with the quotient map
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}
, induces a map of tori
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
, sending a
d
-semistable deformation of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
on the corresponding deformation of the irreducible component
(V_{i},D_{i})
.

Figure 3.2.
Induction step in the proof of Theorem
3.16
.

Theorem 3.16
.

The map
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})\rightarrow\prod_{i}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
is surjective.

Proof.

For every irreducible component
(V_{i},D_{i})
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, define
d(i)
as the smallest nonnegative integer such that there exists a sequence of irreducible components
V_{j_{0}}
,
V_{j_{1}}
,…,
V_{j_{d(i)}}
such that
\dim V_{j_{k}}\cap V_{j_{k+1}}=1
for all
k
,
V_{j_{0}}=V_{i}
, and
V_{j_{d(i)}}
is non-compact. For example, we have
d(i)=0
if and only if
V_{i}
is non-compact. In other words,
d(i)
is the distance following edges in the dual intersection complex
(P,\mathscr{P})
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
between the vertex corresponding to
v_{i}
and the boundary of
P
.

Start with any
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\cup_{i}(V_{i},D_{i})
.
We show below that one can arbitrary deform the components
(V_{i},D_{i})
while rescaling the gluings along the double curves to make
\mathcal{X}_{0}

d
-semistable.
More precisely, we prove by descending induction on
d
that one can arbitrary deform the components
(V_{i},D_{i})
with
d(i)\geq d
while rescaling the gluings along the double curves to keep
\psi_{i}=1
for all
i
such that
V_{i}
is compact and
d(i)\geq d
. This implies Theorem
3.16
when
d=0
.

Since
\mathcal{X}_{0}
has finitely many irreducible components, the set of values of
d(i)
is finite, and so the result is immediate for
d
large enough.
For the induction step, assume that the result holds for
d+1
. In what follows, we show that it holds for
d
.

Let
V_{j}
be a component with
d(j)=d-1
. If
d-1=0
, let
D_{jk_{1}},\cdots,D_{jk_{m}}
be the chain of irreducible components of
D_{j}
, such that
D_{jk_{l}}\cap D_{jk_{l+1}}\neq\emptyset
.
If
d-1>0
, then there is at least one component
D_{jk_{0}}
of
D_{j}
such that
d(k_{0})=d-2
, and denote by

D_{jk_{1}},\cdots,D_{jk_{m}}
the chain of the remaining irreducible components of
D_{j}
– see Figure
3.2
. Note that, for every
1\leq l\leq m
, we have
d(k_{l})\leq d
.

We first deform
D_{jk_{1}}
arbitrarily, which induces a change of
\psi_{k_{1}}
. By Lemma
3.15
, one can keep
\psi_{k_{1}}=1
, by changing the gluing along
D_{jk_{2}}
. Note that this also affects
\psi_{j}
,
\psi_{k_{2}}
, and
\psi_{k_{3}}
.
Next we deform
D_{jk_{2}}
, and we can obtain
\psi_{k_{2}}=1
by changing the gluing along
D_{jk_{3}}
. Note that this does not affect
\psi_{k_{1}}=1
obtained in the previous step. Iterating the process, we end by deforming
D_{jk_{m}}
.
Since
V_{k_{m}}
is adjacent to
V_{k_{0}}
, it follows that we have
d(k_{m})\leq d-1
, and so we do not have to fix
\psi_{k_{m}}
at this point of the induction.
At the end of this process, for every
1\leq l\leq m
such that
d(k_{l})=d
, the corresponding surface
V_{k_{l}}
has been deformed and
\psi_{k_{l}}
has been rescaled to
1
.

We apply the previous process to every component
V_{j}
with
d(j)=d-1
. Every component
V_{i}
with
d(i)=d
is adjacent to a component
V_{j}
with
d(j)=d-1
, and so is ultimately deformed while
\psi_{i}
is set equal to
1
.
In this process, we only change the gluing along double curves of the form
D_{ij}
, with triple points
D_{ijk}
and
D_{ijl}
, such that
d(i)
,
d(j)
,
d(k)
, or
d(l)
are all equal to
d-2
,
d-1
, or
d
. In particular,
by Lemma
3.15
,
the values of
\psi_{i}
with
d(i)>d
are never changed, and so stay equal to their value
1
given by the induction hypothesis.
∎

As in
[
42
, Definition 1.4]
, we call a (possibly non-compact) log Calabi–Yau surface
generic
if it does not contain any internal
(-2)
