-curve. In what follows, we define generic open Kulikov surfaces.
To motivate the definition, we first introduce the notions of M1 and M2 modifications of an open Kulikov surface, following the classical case of Type III Kulikov surfaces – e.g. see
[
7
, Definition 7.15]
.
If
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is an open Kulikov surface, and
C
an internal exceptional curve contained in a component
V_{i}
, intersecting the double locus
V_{i}\cap V_{j}
at the point
p
, then, blowing-down
C
in
V_{i}
, and blowing up
p
in
V_{j}
produces a new open Kulikov surface, called the M1 modification of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
along
C
. Similarly, if
C=V_{i}\cap V_{j}
is an exceptional component of the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, containing the triple points
p_{ijk}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}\cap V_{k}
and
p_{ijl}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}\cap V_{l}
, then, blowing-down
C
in
V_{i}
and
V_{j}
, and blowing-up
p_{ijk}

and
p_{ijl}
in
V_{k}
and
V_{l}
respectively, produces a new open Kulikov surface, called the
M2 modification of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
along
C
. Under an M1 modification, an internal
(-2)
-curve can disappear and be replaced by a particular configuration of curves – see Figure
3.3
, that we call
degenerate
(-2)
-curves
and define as follows.

Figure 3.3.
From an internal
(-2)
-curve to a degenerate
(-2)
-curve by an M1 modification.

Recall from
[
73
, Definition 2.5]
that a stable map
f:C\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}
is
predeformable
if
f(C)
does not contain any stratum of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and, for every node
p=C_{i}\cap C_{j}\in C
with
f(p)
contained in a component
D_{ij}=V_{i}\cap V_{j}
of the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, we have
f(C_{i})\subset V_{i}
,
f(C_{j})\subset V_{j}
, and the contact order of
f(C_{i})
along
D_{ij}
at
f(p)
is equal to the contact order of
f(C_{j})
along
D_{ij}
at
f(p)
.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be an open Kulikov surface. A
degenerate
(-2)
-curve

C
in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is the image of a predeformable genus zero stable map to
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, such that

\sum_{i}C_{i}^{2}=-2
,
where
C_{i}
are the irreducible components of
C
, and

C_{i}^{2}
is the self-intersection of
C_{i}
in the unique irreducible component of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
containing
C_{i}
.

Remark 3.18
.

An internal
(-2)
-curve is a particular example of degenerate
(-2)
-curve. Another degenerate
(-2)
-curve is illustrated in Figure
3.4
. In general, the dual intersection complex
\Sigma(C)
of a degenerate
(-2)
-curve
C
is the image of a tree. If
\Sigma(C)
is simply a point, then
C
is an internal
(-2)
-curves. Else,
\Sigma(C)
contains some univalent vertices which correspond to internal exceptional curves. Iterated M1 modifications along these internal exceptional curves transform
C
into an internal
(-2)
-curve.

Figure 3.4.
A degenerate
(-2)
-curve.

Definition 3.19
.

A
d
-semistable open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is called
generic
if, for every open Kulikov surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
such that there exists a contraction
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}
with exceptional locus a union of toric irreducible components of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, the surface
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}

does not contain any degenerate
(-2)
-curve.

Remark 3.20
.

If
\mathcal{X}_{0}
admits a smoothing
\mathcal{X}
, then the “generic” condition is preserved under replacing
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by the central fiber
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
of a log resolution of a base change of
\mathcal{X}
.

The following is the main result of this section.

Theorem 3.21
.

Any open Kulikov surface admits a generic
d
-semistable locally trivial deformation.

Proof.

First note that the number of conditions to check in Definition
3.19
is countable and that each of them is satisfied on a Zariski open subset in
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
. Hence, it is enough to check that the locus in
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
where each of those conditions is satisfied is non-empty.
By
[
42
, Proposition 4.1]
, a log Calabi–Yau surface
(V_{i},D_{i})
corresponding to a general point of
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L}_{i},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
does not contain any internal
(-2)
-curve.

Now, consider a general degenerate
(-2)
-curve
C=C_{i_{0}}\cup\cdots\cup C_{i_{k}}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
, as in Definition
3.17
. If
C
is the pre-image of a degenerate
(-2)
-curve in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, then we can assume that
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}=\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and there exists an open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
, obtained from
\mathcal{X}_{0}
by a sequence of M1 modifications, and containing an internal
(-2)
-curve. Moreover, it follows from
[
7
, §7C]
that
\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
for
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\prime}
are naturally identified. Hence, the result follows in this case from the previous argument for internal
(-2)
-curves. If
C
is not the pre-image of a degenerate
(-2)
-curve in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, then there is at least one component
D_{ij}
of the double locus of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
which intersects
C
and is contracted in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. On the other hand, the existence of
C
imposes a non-trivial condition on the positions of the internal exceptional curves corresponding to the univalent vertices of the dual complex of
C
, and so defines a closed proper subset of
\prod_{k}\mathrm{Hom}(\mathbb{L}_{k},\mathbb{C}^{\star})
. If we generically change those positions, so that
C
disappears, then it follows from the proof of Theorem
3.16
that one can make
\mathcal{X}_{0}

d
-semistable by changing the gluing along the double locus of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. As
D_{ij}
is contracted in
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, the gluing along
D_{ij}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is not affected, and so the class of
C
remains non-effective.
∎

3.4.
Smoothing of
d
-semistable open Kulikov surfaces

A major result in the context of degenerations of K3 surfaces is the existence of smoothings of d-semistable Type III Kulikov surfaces, shown by Friedman
[
33
]
. In this section, we prove in
Theorem
3.26
the existence of smoothings of
d
-semistable open Kulikov surfaces. Technical subtleties in this setup arise due to the non-compactness of open Kulikov surfaces. Furthermore, as the existence of
(-1)
-forms for Type III Kulikov surface, used in the proof of
[
33
]
, does not have an analog in the framework of open Kulikov surfaces, we have to provide an alternative geometric argument to prove the vanishing of obstructions to smoothing.

3.4.1.
Log smooth deformation theory

In this section, we briefly review definitions coming from log geometry to establish notation for the following section, where we will use the theory of logarithmic deformations of normal crossing varieties, developed by Kawamata–Nawikawa
[
62
]
to describe a smoothing of a
d
-semistable open Kulikov surface. We assume basic familiarity with log geometry, in the sense of
[
58
]
– a comprehensive exposition of log structures can be found in
[
79
]
.

Recall that on a scheme
X
, a
log structure
is given by a sheaf of commutative monoids
\mathcal{M}_{X}
, together with a homomorphism of sheaves
\alpha_{X}:\mathcal{M}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}
between multiplicative monoids, inducing an isomorphism
\alpha_{X}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
. This isomorphism allows us to identify
\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
as a subsheaf of
\mathcal{M}_{X}
. We call a scheme
X
endowed with a log structure a log scheme, and denote it by
X^{\dagger}:=(X,\mathcal{M}_{X})
– we suppress the
\alpha_{X}
from the notation, at situations it is clear what is is. Throughout this paper we assume that all log schemes are fine and saturated
[
79
, I,§1.3]
. The sheaf
\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X}:=\mathcal{M}_{X}/\mathcal{O}_{X}^{\times}
known as the
ghost sheaf
or
characteristic sheaf
, captures the key combinatorial
information about the log structure and in particular, the
tropicalization

\Sigma(X)
of
X
, which is an abstract polyhedral
cone complex – see
[
1
]
, §2.1 for details. In brief, the tropicalization

\Sigma(X)
is given by a collection of cones along with face maps between
them. For every
geometric point
\bar{x}\rightarrow X
, there is one cone
\sigma_{\bar{x}}:=\mathrm{Hom}(\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\bar{x}},\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0})
, and if
\bar{x}
specializes to
\bar{y}
, there is a generization map

\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\bar{y}}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,\bar{x}}
. This leads dually to a map

\sigma_{\bar{x}}\rightarrow\sigma_{\bar{y}}
, and the assumption that our log structures implies this is an inclusion of faces.

An important log structure for us is the
divisorial log structure
, associated to a scheme
X
with a divisor
D\subset X
. This log structure, denoted by
\mathcal{M}_{(X,D)}
, is given by the subsheaf of
\mathcal{O}_{X}
consisting of functions invertible on the complement
X\setminus D
. In the situation when
X=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[Q]
is a toric variety, endowed with the divisorial log structure where
D:=\partial X
given by the toric boundary divisor, the tropicalization
\Sigma(X)=(\mathbb{R}^{n},\Sigma)
corresponds to the toric fan
\Sigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{n}
for
X
.

Given a log scheme
Y^{\dagger}=(Y,\mathcal{M}_{Y})
and a morphism of schemes
f:X\to Y
, one can define an induced log structure, also known as
pull-back log structure
on
X
, which we denote by
f^{*}\mathcal{M}_{Y}
– see
[
13
, Defn.2.16]
. For a monoid
Q

such that
Q^{\times}=\{0\}
, pulling-back the divisorial log structure on the affine toric variety
X=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[Q]
to its torus fixed point
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}
, we obtain the standard log structure on
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}
given by

Q\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\times}\longrightarrow\mathbb{C},\quad(q,a)\longmapsto\begin{cases}a,&q=0\\
0,&q\neq 0.\end{cases}\,.

Throughout this paper, unless stated otherwise, we assume
Q:=\mathbb{N}
and denote the standard log point by

{\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}}^{\dagger}:=(\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C},\mathbb{N}\oplus\mathbb{C}^{\times})
.

A
morphism of log schemes

f^{\dagger}:X^{\dagger}\rightarrow Y^{\dagger}
consists of a scheme theoretic morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
, along with
a map
f^{\flat}:f^{-1}\mathcal{M}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{M}_{X}
compatible with
f^{\#}:f^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{X}
via the structure homomorphisms

\alpha_{X}
and
\alpha_{Y}
. Tropicalization of log schemes is functorial: given a morphism of log schemes
f^{\dagger}:X^{\dagger}\rightarrow Y^{\dagger}
inducing

f_{\mathrm{trop}}:\Sigma(X)\rightarrow\Sigma(Y)
, with a map of cones

\sigma_{\bar{x}}\rightarrow\sigma_{f(\bar{x})}
induced by

\bar{f}^{\flat}:\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{Y,f(\bar{x})}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{M}}_{X,x}
. In the situation, we have a one-parameter degeneration of a scheme
X
, with central fiber
X_{0}
, the divisorial log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X,X_{0}}
induces a log structure
\mathcal{M}_{X_{0}}
on
X_{0}
. In this case, the tropicalization
\Sigma(X_{0})
of the log scheme
(X_{0},\mathcal{M}_{X_{0}})
is the cone
(C(P),C(\mathscr{P}))
over the dual intersection complex
(P,\mathscr{P})
of
X_{0}
– see for instance
[
15
, Proposition 7.4.i]
.

Similarly, as for scheme theoretic morphisms we have the notion of
smoothness
which can be defined using an infinitesimal lifting criterion, for log morphisms, we have the analog of this notion, given by
log smoothness
. We call a morphism log smooth, if and only if it is locally toroidal, in the sense of
[
57
, (3.5)]
. In the following sections, we study the deformation theory of the normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, which satisfies “log smoothness”, since by Theorem
3.21
it is
d
-semistable, and as shown in
[
62
]
and in
[
57
, Theorem 11.2]
, this implies log smoothness. While log smooth deformation theory is developed in a general set-up by Fumiharu Kato
[
57
]
, it suffices for our purposes to consider the normal crossing situation, following
[
62
]
. We do so in the following section.

3.4.2.
Smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{0}

Throughout this section, for every log morphism
f^{\dagger}:X^{\dagger}\rightarrow Y^{\dagger}
, between two log schemes
X
and
Y
we denote by
T_{X^{\dagger}/Y^{\dagger}}

(resp.
\Omega_{X^{\dagger}/Y^{\dagger}}
)
the associated relative log tangent (resp. cotangent) sheaf, which reduces to a vector bundle in the situation when the morphism
f^{\dagger}
is log smooth
[
58
, Proposition 3.10]
.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be a
d
-semistable open Kulikov surface with dual intersection complex
(P,\mathscr{P})
.
There exists a log structure
\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
and a log smooth morphism

(3.2)

{\pi_{0}}^{\dagger}\colon{\mathcal{X}_{0}}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}\,,

to the standard log point, whose tropicalization is the height function

h\colon(C(P),C(\mathscr{P}))\to(\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0},\Sigma_{\mathbb{A}^{1}})
,
given by projection onto the second factor.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is
d
-semistable, by
[
62
, Proposition 1.1]
it admits a log structure which is log smooth over the standard log point. Since by construction
(P,\mathscr{P})
is the dual intersection complex of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, the statement about the tropicalization is immediate by the description of the tropicalization of a map, as explained in §
3.4.1
.
∎

The key ingredient to show the existence of the smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, is the “vanishing result” shown in Lemma
3.25
. Its proof uses the following observation for smooth log Calabi–Yau surfaces.

Lemma 3.23
.

Let
(X,D)
be a smooth log Calabi–Yau surface, and denote by
D=D_{1}+\ldots+D_{n}
the cyclically ordered irreducible components of
D
. Then, the components
\{D_{3},\ldots,,D_{n}\}
are linearly independent in
H^{2}(X,\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

We will show that, if there exists
a_{i}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

(3.3)

a_{3}D_{3}+\ldots a_{n}D_{n}=0

then
a_{i}=0
for all
3\leq i\leq n
. First, taking the intersection with
D_{2}
of both sides of Equation (
3.3
), we get
a_{3}(D_{2}\cdot D_{3})+\ldots a_{n}(D_{2}\cdot D_{n})=0
. Since
D_{2}\cdot D_{3}=1
and
D_{2}\cdot D_{i}=0
for all
3\leq i\leq n
, as
D_{1}+\ldots+D_{n}
is cyclically ordered, this gives us
a_{3}=0
. Thus, Equation (
3.3
) reduces to
a_{4}D_{4}+\ldots a_{n}D_{n}=0
. Now, taking the intersection with
D_{3}
of both sides, analogously as before, we get
a_{4}=0
. Proceeding similarly, eventually we get
a_{i}=0
for all
3\leq i\leq n
.
∎

The following Lemma
3.24
will also be crucial for the proof of the vanishing result, which follows afterwards. First, define

(3.4)

\mathcal{Z}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}\\
v\notin\partial P\end{subarray}}X^{v}

the union of the proper irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
,
and

\partial\mathcal{Z}:=\mathcal{Z}\cap\bigcup_{v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}}X^{v}\subset\mathcal{Z}

the Cartier divisor on
\mathcal{Z}
given by the union of curves in the double locus of
\mathcal{X}^{0}_{\mathscr{P}}
which are in the intersection of a proper irreducible component with a non-proper irreducible component.

Lemma 3.24
.

We have
H^{0}(\mathcal{Z},(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})(-\partial\mathcal{Z}))=0.

Proof.

We adapt to our situation the argument given in the proof of
[
33
, Lemma 5.9]
(where the log cotangent bundle is denoted by
\Lambda^{1}_{X}
) in the context of degenerations of K3 surfaces – see also
[
84
, Theorem 5.2]
for a similar result in the context of degenerations of Enriques surfaces.

As in
[
33
, Corollary 3.6]
or
[
62
, Proof of Theorem 4.2]
, it follows from the definition of the log cotangent bundle that we have a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}/\tau_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0\,,

where
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
is the usual cotangent sheaf of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
,
\tau_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
is the torsion subsheaf of
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
, and the cokernel
F
sits via the Poincaré residue map into the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}\rightarrow F\rightarrow 0\,,

induced by the decomposition
\mathcal{X}_{0}=\bigcup_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}X^{v}
into
irreducible components.
Using the exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{0}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}}\rightarrow 0\,,

we rewrite
F
as

F=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}}\right)\,.

In restriction to
\mathcal{Z}
, we obtain the short exact sequence

(3.5)

0\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}/\tau_{\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{Z}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 0\,,

where
\tau_{\mathcal{Z}}
is the torsion subsheaf of
\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
, and the cokernel
G
sits via the Poincaré residue map into the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}}\oplus\bigoplus_{v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}\cap\mathcal{Z}}\rightarrow G\rightarrow 0\,,

where
\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[1]}
is the set of vertices
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
such that
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\subset\mathcal{Z}
, that is,
v\notin\partial P
.
One can also describe
G
as

(3.6)

G=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[1]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[2]}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}}\right)\,,

where
\mathscr{P}_{Z}^{[1]}
is the set of edges
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
such that
X^{e}\subset\mathcal{Z}
, that is
e\not\subset\partial P
, and

\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[2]}
is the set of faces
f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}
whose three edges are in
\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[1]}
.
We also denote by
\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}
(resp.
\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\partial\mathcal{Z}}
) the set of edges
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}

such that
X^{e}\subset\mathcal{Z}
and
X^{e}\not\subset\partial\mathcal{Z}

(resp.
X^{e}\subset\mathcal{Z}
and
X^{e}\subset\partial\mathcal{Z}
).

For every vertex
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, the irreducible component
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a proper log Calabi–Yau surface, so
H^{0}(X^{v},\Omega)\subset H^{1}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})
by the Hodge decomposition, and so we have
H^{0}(X^{v},\Omega)=0
since

H^{1}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})=0
. On the other hand, for every
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
, we have
X^{e}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and so
H^{0}(X^{e},\Omega_{X^{e}})=0
. Hence, the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}}/\tau_{Z}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}}\Omega_{X^{v}}\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}\Omega_{X^{e}}\rightarrow 0

implies that we have
H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}}/\tau_{Z})=0

and

\displaystyle H^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}}/\tau_{Z})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}}H^{1}(X^{v},\Omega_{X^{v}})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}H^{1}(X^{e},\Omega_{X^{e}})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}}H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}H^{2}(X^{e},\mathbb{C})\right)\,.

Thus, the long exact sequence in cohomology associated to the short exact sequence (
3.5
) takes the form

0\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{X}_{0},G)\rightarrow\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}}H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})\,.

It follows from the exact sequence in Equation (
3.5
) and from the description of
G
in Equation (
3.6
) that we have an exact sequence

(3.7)

0\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathcal{Z},(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})(-\partial\mathcal{Z}))\rightarrow H\xrightarrow{\delta}\bigoplus_{v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}}H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})\,,

where

H=\mathrm{Ker}\left(\bigoplus_{e\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}^{[1]}}H^{0}(X^{e},\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}})\rightarrow\bigoplus_{f\in\mathscr{P}_{\mathcal{Z}}^{[2]}}H^{0}(X^{f},\mathcal{O}_{X^{f}})\right)\,,

and the map
\delta
in (
3.7
) is induced by the maps

\displaystyle H^{0}(X^{e},\mathcal{O}_{X^{e}})\simeq\mathbb{C}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\longmapsto[X^{e}]

for every
v
and
e
such that
e
is adjacent to
v
, that is, such that
X^{e}\subset X^{v}
.

To prove Lemma
3.24
, we have to show that

H^{0}(\mathcal{Z},(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})(-\partial\mathcal{Z}))=0
,
that is, by (
3.7
), that the map
\delta
is injective. Explicitly, an element of
H

is a sequence
(a_{e})_{e\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial Z}}
of numbers
a_{e}\in\mathbb{C}
, satisfying the balancing condition
a_{e_{1}}+a_{e_{2}}+a_{e_{3}}=0
each time we have three edges
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial Z}
bounding a triangle
f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. Let
(a_{e})_{e\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial Z}}
be in the kernel of
\delta
, that is, that the image of

\sum_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}a_{e}[X^{e}]
in
H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})
is zero for every
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
. For every vertex
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, define the
distance to the boundary

d(v)
as
d(v)=0
if
v\in\partial P
, and else as the smallest integer such that there exists a sequence

(e_{1},\dots,e_{d(v)})
of edges
e_{i}\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
such that
e_{1}
is adjacent to
v
,
e_{i+1}
is adjacent to
e_{i}
, and
e_{d(v)}\in\partial P
.
We will prove that
a_{e}=0
for all
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
,
by showing by induction on
d(v)\geq 0
that, for every vertex
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, we have
a_{e}=0
for all edges
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}
adjacent to
v
.

For the initialization of the induction, note that if
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
is such that
d(v)=0
, then
v\in\partial P
, so we have
e\notin\mathscr{P}^{1}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}
for every edge
e
adjacent to
v
, and so the result is empty is this case. For the induction step, let
d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, and assume that the result holds for all vertices
v^{\prime}
with
d(v^{\prime})<d
. Let
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}_{\mathcal{Z}}
such that
d(v)=d
.
Then, there exists an edge
e_{1}
adjacent to
v
which is also adjacent to a vertex
v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
with
d(v^{\prime})<d
. Let
f\in\mathscr{P}^{[2]}
be a triangle with side
e_{1}
, and denote by
e_{2}
the other side adjacent to
v
, and by
e_{3}
the other side adjacent to
v^{\prime}
. By the induction hypothesis, we have
a_{e_{1}}=a_{e_{3}}=0
, and so it follows from the balancing condition
a_{e_{1}}+a_{e_{2}}+a_{e_{3}}=0
that we also have
a_{e_{2}}=0
. Therefore, the restriction of
\sum_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}a_{e}[X^{e}]
to
X^{v}
is a linear combination of the boundary divisors of
X^{v}
not containing the consecutive components
X^{e_{1}}
and
X^{e_{2}}
. Hence, by Lemma
3.23
, the condition that

\sum_{e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{Z}\setminus\partial\mathcal{Z}}}a_{e}[X^{e}]
is
zero in
H^{2}(X^{v},\mathbb{C})
implies that
a_{e}=0
for all edges
e
adjacent to
v
. This concludes the proof that
\delta
is injective, and so the proof of Lemma
3.24
.
∎

Now, we are ready to prove the vanishing result.

Lemma 3.25
.

We have

H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}})=0\,
where
T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}
is the log tangent bundle of

\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}
over
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}
.

Proof.

Recall that the affinization map
f_{0}:\mathcal{X}_{0}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is a projective surjective birational morphism. Since
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
is affine, then, by
[
51
, Proposition III.8.5]
(a degenerate case of the Leray spectral sequence), we have

H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}})=H^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0},R^{2}(f_{0})_{\star}\,T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}})\,.

Fibers of
f_{0}
over points in

\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
distinct from the central point
x_{0}

are of dimension at most one, and so, by
[
51
, Corollary III.11.2]
, the sheaf

R^{2}(f_{0})_{\star}\,T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}
is supported on the central point
x_{0}
.
Hence, it is enough to show that its stalk at
x_{0}
is
0
:

(3.8)

\left(R^{2}(f_{0})_{\star}\,T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}\right)_{x_{0}}=0\,.

As the sheaf
R^{2}(f_{0})_{\star}\,T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}
is supported
x_{0}
, the formal completion of the stalk at
x_{0}
is equal to the stalk at
x_{0}
itself, and so, by the theorem on formal functions
[
51
, Theorem 11.1]
, we have

\left(R^{2}(f_{0})_{\star}\,T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}\right)_{x_{0}}=\varprojlim_{k}H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}})\,,

where for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
,
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}
is the projective subscheme of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
obtained by taking the scheme-theoretic preimage by
f_{0}
of the
k
-th infinitesimal neighborhood of
x_{0}
in
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
. In particular, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the reduced underlying scheme of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}
is the fiber

\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}=f_{0}^{-1}(x_{0})
. As all proper 2-dimensional irreducible of components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
are contained in
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}
, it follows that, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the schemes

\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}
and

\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}
only differ along the finitely many curves where
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}
intersect the non-proper irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. In particular, the kernel and cokernel of the restriction map

T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}}\longrightarrow T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}}

are supported in dimension
1
, so have vanishing
H^{2}
, and so the corresponding long exact sequences in cohomology imply that, for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(k)}})=H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}})\,.

Thus, in order to prove the vanishing in Equation (
3.8
), and so Lemma
3.25
, it is enough to show that

(3.9)

H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}})=0\,.

The irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}
are the proper surfaces
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
, indexed by the interior vertices
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, and the proper curves

X^{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
indexed by the set
\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}}
of interior edges
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
connecting pairs of boundary vertices. We denote by

\mathcal{X}^{(0)}_{0}=\mathcal{Z}\cup\mathcal{E}

the corresponding decomposition, where

\mathcal{Z}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}\\
v\notin\partial P\end{subarray}}X^{v}\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{and}\,\,\,\,\,\,\mathcal{E}:=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}_{\mathcal{E}}\end{subarray}}X^{e}\,.

Note that the intersection
\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{E}
is a finite union of triple points of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.
Tensoring the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}^{(0)}_{0}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow 0

with the locally free log tangent bundle
T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}
produces a short exact sequence

0\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}^{(0)}_{0}}\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}\oplus T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{E}}\rightarrow 0\,.

Using the vanishing of the
H^{1}
and
H^{2}
groups implied by
\dim\mathcal{E}=1
and
\dim\mathcal{Z}\cap\mathcal{E}=0
in the corresponding long exact sequence in cohomology, we obtain

(3.10)

H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{(0)}})=H^{2}(\mathcal{Z},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})\,.

By Lemma
3.8
, the
dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathscr{X}_{0}}
is trivial, and so, by adjunction, the dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathcal{Z}}
of
\mathcal{Z}
is given by the line bundle

\omega_{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}(-\partial\mathcal{Z})
.
Hence, by Serre duality on
\mathcal{Z}
, and using that
T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}}
is locally free on
\mathcal{Z}
, we obtain

(3.11)

H^{2}(\mathcal{Z},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})=H^{0}(\mathcal{Z},(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})(-\partial\mathcal{Z}))^{\vee}\,.

By Lemma
3.24
, we have
H^{0}(\mathcal{Z},(\Omega_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}}|_{\mathcal{Z}})(-\partial\mathcal{Z}))=0
, and so we deduce using Equations (
3.10
)-(
3.11
) that the vanishing of Equation (
3.9
) holds, and this concludes the proof of Lemma
3.25
.
∎

Finally, we are ready to state the existence of a smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
.

Theorem 3.26
.

Let
\mathcal{X}_{0}
be a
d
-semistable open Kulikov surface.
There exists a formal smoothing
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
over the formal disk
\Delta=\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}\llbracket t\rrbracket
, such that the following holds:

i)

The total space
\mathcal{X}
is smooth.

ii)

The central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a normal crossing divisor in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}
.

iii)

The log smooth morphism

{\pi_{0}}^{\dagger}\colon{\mathcal{X}_{0}}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{M}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})\longrightarrow\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}

in (
3.22
) is given by the restriction to the central fiber
{\mathcal{X}_{0}}
of the log morphism

\pi^{\dagger}\colon\mathcal{X}^{\dagger}=(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{M}_{(\mathcal{X},\partial\mathcal{X})})\longrightarrow\Delta^{\dagger}=(\Delta,\mathcal{M}_{(\Delta,{0})})\,,

induced by the morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
.

Proof.

Since the obstruction group
H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},T_{\mathcal{X}_{0}^{\dagger}/\mathrm{Spec}\mathbb{C}^{\dagger}})
vanishes by Lemma
3.25
, the result follows by log smooth deformation theory as in
[
62
, Corollary 2.4]
.
∎

3.5.
Open Kulikov degenerations

We define below open Kulikov degenerations as “semistable smoothings” of a generic open Kulikov surfaces.

Definition 3.27
.

An
open Kulikov degeneration
is a proper morphism

\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta:=\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[\![t]\!]
of formal schemes satisfying the following conditions.

i)

The total space
\mathcal{X}
is smooth.

ii)

The central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a reduced normal crossing divisor in
\mathcal{X}
.

iii)

The central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a generic open Kulikov surface – as in Definition
3.19
.

Remark 3.28
.

If one replaces condition iii) in Definition
3.27

by “the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a Kulikov surface”, one recovers the notion of Type III Kulikov degeneration of K3 surfaces
[
35
,
70
,
81
]
.

By definition, the central fiber of an open Kulikov degeneration is a
d
-semistable generic open Kulikov surface. Conversely, by Theorem
3.26
, every
d
-semistable generic open Kulikov surface is the central fiber of an open Kulikov degeneration.

Recall from §
3.2
that every open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
has an affinization
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
. The following result describes the affinization of open Kulikov degenerations.

Theorem 3.29
.

Let
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
be a semistable smoothing of a generic open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. Then, the affinization
\overline{\mathcal{X}}\coloneqq\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}})
is a smoothing of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}\coloneqq\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{0}})
, and has Gorenstein canonical singularities. Moreover,
\mathcal{X}
has trivial canonical line bundle, and the affinization morphism
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is a crepant resolution of singularities.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10
, we have
H^{1}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})=0
, and so it follows from

[
96
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also
[
26
, Theorem 3.1]
) that
f
is a flat deformation of
f_{0}
.
To prove that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
has Gorenstein canonical singularities, first note that
\mathcal{X}
has trivial canonical line bundle – this can be shown analogously as in
[
33
, Theorem 5.10, 3)]
, where a similar result is proved in the context of degenerations of K3 surfaces. Hence, since
\mathcal{X}
is a smooth threefold with trivial canonical class, by
[
60
, Corollary 1.5]
it follows that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
has Gorenstein canonical singularities.
Note that
[
60
, Corollary 1.5]
is stated in the context of projective morphism between normal algebraic varieties. In this context, the result follows from the base point free theorem
[
60
, Theorem 1.3]
.
The same argument shows that the result for projective morphisms of formal schemes follows from the corresponding version of the base point free theorem
in
[
75
, §11]
.

It remains to show that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}\rightarrow\Delta
is a smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. It is enough to show that the rigid-analytic generic fiber
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}
of
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is smooth.
Denote by
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}
the rigid-analytic generic fiber of
\mathcal{X}
, which is smooth since
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is smoothing. Standard results on birational geometry of surfaces generalize to rigid-analytic surfaces by
[
95
, §5]
. Hence, it follows from the triviality of the canonical class of
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}
has trivial that the birational map
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\eta}
can only contract
(-2)
-curves. Hence, it is enough to show that
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}
does not contain any
(-2)
-curve. If
C_{\eta}
were a
(-2)
-curve in
\mathcal{X}_{\eta}
, then up to replacing
\mathcal{X}
by a log resolution
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
of a base change,
C_{\eta}

would admit a model with central fiber a curve
C_{0}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
containing no 0-dimensional stratum of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{0}
. Such a curve
C_{0}
would necessarily be either an internal
(-2)
-curve or a degenerate
(-2)
-curve as in Definition
3.17
, in contradiction with the assumption that the open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is generic as in Definition
3.19
.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

Since
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is an affine formal smoothing, it follows from Elkik’s theorem

[
29
, Theorem 7, p582]

– see also
[
92
, Proposition 3.3.1]
,
that
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is algebraizable over
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\![t]\!]
.
Moreover,
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is a formal blow-up, and so is algebraizable in the category of algebraic spaces over
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\![t]\!]
by

[
19
, Theorem 3.2]
. Since
H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})=0
, and
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is projective, it follows that
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is in fact algebraizable in the category of schemes over
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\![t]\!]
.

We will mainly work with quasi-projective open Kulikov degenerations. The following result gives several equivalent characterizations of this notion.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
be an open Kulikov degeneration with central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. The
following are equivalent:

i)

The open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is quasi-projective.

ii)

The morphism
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is quasi-projective.

(iii)

The affinization morphism
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is projective.

Proof.

The only non-immediate implication is i) implies ii). By Lemma
3.10
, we have
H^{2}(\mathcal{X}_{0},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{X}_{0}})=0
, so there are no obstructions to formal deformations over
\Delta
of an ample line bundle on
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, and so the result follows.
∎

Finally, we show that the compact irreducible components of the central fiber of a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration are positive log Calabi–Yau surfaces.

Definition 3.32
.

A smooth log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D)
is called
positive
if the intersection matrix
(D_{i}\cdot D_{j})
of the irreducible components
D_{i}
of
D
is not negative semi-definite, that is, if there exists integers
a_{i}
such that
(\sum_{i}a_{i}D_{i})^{2}>0
.

Lemma 3.33
.

Let
(Y,D=\sum_{i}D_{i})
be a smooth log Calabi–Yau surface. Then, the following are equivalent:

i)

(Y,D)
is positive.

ii)

The complement
U=Y\setminus D
is the minimal resolution of an affine surface with at worst Du Val singularities.

iii)

There exist positive integers
b_{j}

such that
(\sum_{j}b_{j}D_{j})\cdot D_{i}>0
for all
i
.

iv)

There exists
0<c_{i}<1
such that
-(K_{Y}+\sum_{i}c_{i}D_{i})
is big and nef.

v)

-K_{Y}
is big.

Proof.

The equivalence between (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv) is the equivalence between (1.1), (1.2), (1.3) and (1.4) in
[
41
, Lemma 6.9]
.

If
W=-(K_{Y}+\sum_{i}c_{i}D_{i})
is big and nef, then

-K_{Y}=W+\sum_{i}c_{i}D_{i}
is the sum of a big divisor with an effective divisor, and so
-K_{Y}
big. This shows that (iv) implies (v).
Finally, we show that (v) implies (iii). If
-K_{Y}
is big, then
\sum_{i}D_{i}=-K_{Y}
is big, and so the result follows from the proof that (1.1) implies (1.2) in the proof of
[
41
, Lemma 6.9]
.
∎

Theorem 3.34
.

The normalization of every compact irreducible component
(X^{i},\partial X^{i})
of the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
of a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is a positive smooth log Calabi–Yau surface.

Proof.

By assumption, the open Kulikov degeneration
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is quasi-projective. Hence, by Lemma
3.31
, the contraction
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
is projective.
Thus, by the relative Kodaira Lemma applied to
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\overline{\mathcal{X}}
, there exists an effective divisor
F
on
\mathcal{X}
such that
-F
is
f
-ample. Write
F=a_{i}X^{i}+G
, where
a_{i}
is an integer and
G
is a divisor not containing
X^{i}
.
As
X^{i}
is a compact irreducible component of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
,
X^{i}
is contracted by
f
to a point and so it follows from the Negativity Lemma
[
67
, Lemma 3.39]
that
a_{i}>0
.
Restricting
F
to
X^{i}
, we obtain:

F|_{X^{i}}=a_{i}X^{i}|_{X^{i}}+G|_{X^{i}}\,.

As
K_{\mathcal{X}}=0
, it follows by adjunction that the normal bundle to
X^{i}
in
\mathcal{X}
is
K_{X^{i}}
, and so
X^{i}|_{X^{i}}=K_{X^{i}}
. Hence, we have

(3.12)

-K_{X^{i}}=\frac{1}{a_{i}}(-F|_{X^{i}}+G|_{X^{i}})\,.

As
X^{i}
is contracted by
f
and
-F
is
f
-ample, it follows that
-F|_{X^{i}}
is ample on
X^{i}
. On the other hand, as
G
does not contain
X^{i}
,
G|_{X^{i}}
is effective. Therefore,
-F|_{X^{i}}+G|_{X^{i}}
is the sum of an ample divisor with an effective divisor, and so is big. Hence,
-K_{X^{i}}
is big by the Equation (
3.12
) above and using the fact that
a_{i}>0
. Finally, we conclude that
(X^{i},\partial X^{i})
is positive using that (v) implies (i) in Lemma
3.33

∎

3.6.
Mori fans of quasi-projective open Kulikov degenerations

In this section, we show that the total space of a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration is a (relative) Mori dream space, as in
[
53
]
for the absolute case and
[
12
,
80
]
for the relative case. Consequently, we attach to any quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration the corresponding Mori fan.

Definition 3.35
.

Let
f:V\to W
be a projective morphism between formal schemes
V,W
. Then,
V
is called a
Mori dream space over W
, if the following conditions are satisfied:

i)

V
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial,
and
\mathrm{Pic}(V/W)
is a finitely generated abelian group.

ii)

The relative nef cone
\mathrm{Nef}(V/W)
is generated by finitely many semi-ample divisors.

iii)

There is a finite collection of small
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial modifications
f_{i}:V\dashrightarrow V_{i}
such that each
V_{i}
satisfies
2)
and the Movable cone of relative moving divisors
\mathrm{Mov}(V/W)
is a union of the cones
f_{i}^{*}(\mathrm{Nef}(V_{i}/W))
.

Proposition 3.36
.

The total space of a quasi-projective open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}
is a Mori dream space over
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
3.29
, using
[
23
, Corollary 1.3.2]
adapted to the formal setup – see
[
75
]
.
∎

In what follows we describe the Mori fan associated to an open Kulikov degeneration. For any Mori dream space

V
over
W
, where
V
and
W
are normal algebraic varieties with a projective morphism
f:V\to W
there is an associated fan in
\mathrm{Pic}(V/W)_{\mathbb{R}}
, known as the Mori fan, with support the cone of
f
-effective divisors,
\mathrm{Eff}(V/W)

[
53
,
80
]
. Using formal Mori theory as in
[
75
]
, one can similarly construct a Mori fan for a Mori dream space
V
over
W
, where
V
and
W
are formal schemes and
f:V\rightarrow W
is a projective morphism.
The maximal dimensional cones of the Mori fan are in one-to-one correspondence with the birational contractions
g:V\dashrightarrow V^{\prime}
over
W
where
V^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-factorial. For such a birational contraction, the corresponding maximal cone of the Mori fan is given by

g^{*}\mathrm{Nef}(V^{\prime}/W)+\mathrm{Ex}(g)
,
where
\mathrm{Ex}(g)
is the cone in
\mathrm{Pic}(V/W)_{\mathbb{R}}
spanned by all
g
-exceptional divisors.
For a birational projective morphism
f:V\to W
, we have
\mathrm{Eff}(V/W)=\mathrm{Pic}(V/W)_{\mathbb{R}}
, and so the Mori fan is a complete fan in
\mathrm{Pic}(V/W)_{\mathbb{R}}
in this case.
Given an open Kulikov degeneration, with affinization
f\colon\mathcal{X}\to\overline{\mathcal{X}}
,
\mathcal{X}
is a Mori dream space over
\overline{\mathcal{X}}
by Proposition
3.36
, and so we have an associated complete Mori fan in
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
, denoted by

\mathrm{MF}(\mathcal{X}/\overline{\mathcal{X}})
in the following sections.

4.
Semistable mirrors of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces

In this section, we construct in
§
4.2

a quasi-projective open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, associated to a polarized log Calabi–Yau
(Y,D,L)
and a choice of Symington polytope
P
endowed with a good polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
. We describe
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
as a union of suitable “deformations” of toric varieties determined by the polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
. The necessary results in deformation theory of log Calabi–Yau surfaces are first established in §
4.1
.
Finally, we define in §
4.3
the semistable mirror to
(Y,D,L)
as an
open Kulikov degeneration
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
obtained as the total space of a semistable smoothing of the open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
.

4.1.
Deformation theory of log Calabi–Yau surfaces

The results in deformation theory of surfaces established below will be used in the next section §
4.2
in the construction of the open Kulikov surface

\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
X
be a normal projective surface with isolated singularities and let
D
be an effective divisor on
X
. If we have
H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee})=0
, then there are no local-to-global obstructions to deformations of the pair
(X,D)
.

Proof.

The result is well-known if
D
is empty, see for example
[
82
, Theorem 4.10]
,
[
76
, Lemma 1]
, or
[
97
, Proposition 6.4]
. On the other hand, if
D
is non-empty but
X
is smooth, a proof can be found in
[
34
, Proposition 3.5]
. Our proof below of the general case is based on a combination of the techniques used for these two known special cases.

Let
U
be the non-empty open subset of
X
where both
X
and
D
are smooth, and denote by
j:U\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion. By assumption, the complement
X\setminus D
consists of isolated points in the surface
X
, and so if of codimension two.
Consider the complex
A:=(j_{\star}T_{X}|_{U}\rightarrow j_{\star}N_{D|U})
, where
T_{X}|_{U}
is the restriction to
U
of the tangent sheaf
T_{X}
, placed in degree
0
, and
N_{D|U}=\mathcal{O}_{D\cap U}(D\cap U)
is the normal sheaf to
D\cap U
in
D\cap U
, placed in degree
1
. Using
as in the proof of
[
82
, Theorem 4.10]
a Čech cover of
X
by affine subsets centered on the isolated singularities and with double and triple intersections contained in
U
, we deduce that if the hypercohomology group
\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,A)

vanishes, then there are no local-to-global obstructions to deformations of
(X,D)
. When
X
is smooth, details of this argument can be found in
[
88
, Proposition 8]
.

Therefore, it remains to show that
H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee})=0
implies that
\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,A)=0
.
Consider the short exact sequence

0\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{U}(-D\cap U)\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{U}\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{D\cap U}\rightarrow 0

defining
D\cap U
in
U
. As
T_{X}|_{U}
is locally free, tensoring by
T_{X}|_{U}
,
and then applying
j_{\star}
,
we obtain an exact sequence of the form

0\rightarrow j_{\star}T_{X}|_{U}(-D\cap U)\rightarrow j_{\star}T_{X}|_{U}\rightarrow j_{\star}T_{X}|_{D\cap U}\rightarrow P\rightarrow 0\,,

where
P
is a sheaf supported on
X\setminus U
. Note that as
T_{X}|_{U}(-D\cap U)
is locally free on
U
, we have

j_{\star}T_{X}|_{U}(-D\cap U)=(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee}
.
We split the above long exact sequence into two short exact sequences:

(4.1)

0\rightarrow(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee}\rightarrow j_{\star}T_{X}|_{U}\rightarrow L\rightarrow 0

and

(4.2)

0\rightarrow L\rightarrow j_{\star}T_{X}|_{D\cap U}\rightarrow P\rightarrow 0\,.

Consider the complex

B:=(L\longrightarrow j_{\star}N_{D|U})

obtained by restricting to
L
the natural morphism
(j_{\star}T_{X}|_{D\cap U}\longrightarrow j_{\star}N_{D|U})
.
Restriction to
D
induces a map of complexes
A\rightarrow B
.
By construction, we have an exact sequence of complexes

0\rightarrow(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee}\rightarrow A\rightarrow B\rightarrow 0\,,

and so a corresponding long exact sequence in hypercohomology containing

\cdots\rightarrow H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee})\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,A)\rightarrow\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,B)\rightarrow\cdots\,.

Hence, it is enough to show that
\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,B)=0
in order to show the desired result that
H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(-D))^{\vee\vee})=0
implies
\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,A)=0
.

The kernel
\mathcal{H}^{0}(B)
of
B
is supported in dimension one, so we have

H^{2}(X,\mathcal{H}^{0}(B))=0
, and the cokernel
\mathcal{H}^{1}(B)
of
B
is supported in
X\setminus U
, that is in dimension zero, and so we have also

H^{1}(X,\mathcal{H}^{1}(B))=0
.
It follows from the hypercohomology spectral sequence that
\mathbb{H}^{2}(X,B)=0
, and this concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
X
be a normal projective surface with isolated quotient singularities and let
D\in|-K_{X}|
be an effective reduced anticanonical divisor. Assume that we have
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
. Then, there are no local-to-global obstructions to deformations of
(X,D)
.

Proof.

The following argument is inspired by the proof of

[
76
, Theorem 21]
.
By Lemma
4.1
, it is enough to show that
H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee})=0
.
A normal surface being Cohen-Macaulay, the reflexive canonical sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X})
is the dualizing sheaf of
X
, and so by Serre duality we have

H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee})=\mathrm{Hom}((T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee},\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee}\,.

Let
j:U\hookrightarrow X
be the open immersion of the smooth locus of
X
. As
X
is normal and
X\setminus U
is of codimension two, the functors
j_{\star}
and
j^{\star}
are inverse equivalences between the category of coherent reflexive sheaves on
X
and the coherent reflexive sheaves on
U

[
89
,
Lemma 0EBJ
]
. As both
(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X})
are reflexive, it follows that

\mathrm{Hom}((T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee},\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}))=\mathrm{Hom}(j^{*}(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee},j^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}))\,.

As
U
is the smooth locus of
X
, the restrictions to
U
of
T_{X}
and
\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X})
are locally free, and so

\mathrm{Hom}(j^{*}(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee},j^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X}(K_{X}))=\mathrm{Hom}(\mathcal{O}_{U},j^{*}\Omega_{X})=H^{0}(U,j^{*}\Omega_{X})\,.

Using
j_{\star}
to come back on
X
, we have

H^{0}(U,j^{*}\Omega_{X})=H^{0}(X,j_{*}j^{\star}\Omega_{X})=H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\vee\vee})\,.

Finally, as
X
has quotient singularities, it follows from Steenbrink Hodge theory for orbifolds
[
90
, §1]
that
\dim H^{0}(X,\Omega_{X}^{\vee\vee})=\dim H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
. Hence, it follows that

\dim H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee})=\dim H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})\,,

and so
H^{2}(X,(T_{X}(K_{X}))^{\vee\vee})=0
if
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
.
∎

4.2.
Open Kulikov surfaces from polarized log Calabi-Yau surfaces

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface and
P
a Symington polytope of
(Y,D,L)
defined by a good toric model
(\overline{Y},\overline{D},\overline{L})

with toric momentum polytope
\overline{P}
, as in §
2
. In this section, we describe the construction, for every good polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
of
\overline{P}
as in §
2.3
, of a quasi-projective open Kulikov surface

\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, which is unique up to locally trivial deformations.

Throughout this section we denote by
M\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
the character lattice for
\overline{Y}
, so that
\overline{P}\subseteq M_{\mathbb{R}}
, where
M_{\mathbb{R}}=M\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
denotes the real vector space associated to
M
.
We first consider the Mumford toric degeneration

\pi_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}:\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

associated to the toric polytope
\overline{P}
endowed with the polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
. As in
[
78
, §3]
,

\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is the 3-dimensional toric variety with fan
C(\overline{\mathscr{P}})

in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
consisting of the cones

C(\sigma)=\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\cdot(\sigma\times\{1\})\subset M_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

over the cells
\sigma\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
.
Moreover, the toric morphism

\pi_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\colon\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}

is induced by the projection
M_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
onto the second factor.
By Definition
2.13
of a good polyhedral decomposition,
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
is a regular decomposition, and so
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a quasi-projective toric variety.

Since all cells
\sigma\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}
are bounded, the so called
asymptotic cone
in
[
78
]
consists of a single point, the origin in
M_{\mathbb{R}}\times\mathbb{R}
, and hence the fibers

\pi_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{-1}(t)
for
t\neq 0

are isomorphic to the algebraic torus
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
.
The central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}\coloneqq\pi_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{-1}(0)
is a union of irreducible components
X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}
, indexed by the vertices
v\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{[0]}
, which are toric surfaces with fan the star of
v
in
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
:

\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}=\bigcup_{v\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{[0]}}X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}\,.

For every vertex
v\in\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{[0]}
, we denote by
\partial X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}

the union of intersections of
X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}

with the other irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}
, which is also the union of toric divisors of
X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}
.

We consider below the Symington polytope
P
, endowed with the polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
induced by the restriction of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
to
P
, and we describe a union
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
of log Calabi–Yau surfaces obtained as a deformation of
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}
, and with irreducible components
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}^{v}
indexed by the vertices
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
. We do this in four steps.

STEP I: As a first step, for every vertex
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, we define a toric surface
\widetilde{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
by deformation of the union
\bigcup_{v^{\prime}\in pr^{-1}(v)}X^{v^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}

of irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}
, where
pr:\overline{\mathscr{P}}^{[0]}\rightarrow\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
is the natural projection coming from the description of the Symington of
P
as a quotient of
\overline{P}\setminus\cup_{ij}\mathrm{Int}(\Delta_{ij})
.
If
pr^{-1}(v)
is of cardinality one, with
pr^{-1}(v)=\{v^{\prime}\}
, then we set simply
\widetilde{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}:=X^{v^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
.
If
pr^{-1}(v)
is of cardinality strictly bigger than one, then
pr^{-1}(v)=\{v^{\prime}_{1},\dots,v^{\prime}_{r}\}
,
where for every
1\leq k\leq r-1
,
v^{\prime}_{k}
and
v^{\prime}_{k+1}
are opposite integral points on the interior edges of a triangle
\Delta_{i_{k}j_{k}}
. As the good decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
is a standard decomposition in restriction to
\Delta_{ij}
, as in Definition
2.13
, it follows that each toric surface
X^{v_{k}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}

has a map to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
induced by the opposite rays in its fan coming from the interior edge of
\Delta_{i_{k}j_{k}}
or
\Delta_{i_{k-1}j_{k-1}}
containing
v_{k}^{\prime}
. These maps
f_{k}:X^{v_{k}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}
are generically
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibrations if
v_{k}^{\prime}
is contained in the interior of
\overline{P}
, and generically
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-fibrations if
v_{k}^{\prime}
is on the boundary of
\overline{P}
.
Moreover, for every
1\leq k\leq r-1
, there is an edge in
\mathscr{P}^{[1]}

contained in
\Delta_{i_{k}j_{k}}
and connecting the vertices
v_{k}^{\prime}
and
v_{k+1}^{\prime}
. This edge corresponds to the intersection
F_{k}:=X^{v_{k}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\cap X^{v_{k+1}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
, and is both a fiber of
f_{k}
in
X^{v_{k}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
and a fiber of
f_{k+1}
in
X^{v_{k+1}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
.

Figure 4.1.
Deformation of the toric intersection complex
of
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}},0}
into the non-toric intersection complex of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
.

Hence,
\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}X^{v_{k}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a chain of fibered surfaces, glued along common fibers. The double locus of such a normal crossing union of toric surfaces can be smoothed by a toric degeneration defined by a polyhedral decomposition of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
as in Figure
4.1
. We define
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v}
as the toric surface given by the general fiber of this deformation. The fan of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v}
can be described explicitly as follows. First we draw the fan of
X^{v_{1}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
such that the opposite rays defining
f_{1}
are horizontal and such that the ray corresponding to
F_{1}
is contained in the upper-half plane. We also draw the fan of

X^{v_{r}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
such that the opposite rays defining
f_{r}
are horizontal and such that the ray corresponding to
F_{r-1}
is contained in the lower-half plane and parallel to the ray corresponding to
F_{1}
in the fan of
X^{v_{1}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
.
Then, the fan of
\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v}
is equal to the fan of
X^{v_{1}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
in restriction to the lower half-plane, and equal to the fan of
X^{v_{r}^{\prime}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
in restriction to the upper half-plane.

STEP II: As a second step, for each vertex
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, we describe a deformation of the toric pair
(\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})
into a log Calabi–Yau pair
(X_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial X_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})
.
Recall by Construction
2.10
, the Symington polytope
P
is obtained from
\overline{P}
, by cutting out triangles
\Delta_{ij}
, with one edge in the boundary of
\overline{P}
and with an interior vertex
p_{ij}
.
For every
i,j
such that
v=pr(p_{ij})
,
there is a face
F_{ij}
of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}

containing
v
and contained in the triangle
\Delta_{ij}
. The face
F_{ij}

defines a 2-dimensional cone in the star of
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
at
v
, that is, in the fan of
X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}
, and so corresponds to a toric fixed point
x_{ij}
of
X_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}^{v}
.
This point is not smoothed by the deformation in Step I, and so also defines a toric fixed point in the toric surface
\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v}
that we still denote by
x_{ij}
.
We denote by
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})

the analytic germ of the surface pair
(\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})
around
x_{ij}
.
As the triangles
\Delta_{ij}
have equal lattice height and lattice base,
it follows from
[
11
, §7.3]
that
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})

is the germ of the origin in an affine toric variety of the form

\mathbb{A}^{2}_{u,v}/\frac{1}{n^{2}}(1,an-1)\,,

with

n\geq 1
and
\mathrm{gcd}(a,n)=1
.
If
n=1
, then
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})
is the germ of the origin in
(\mathbb{A}^{2},\{uv=0\})
.
If
n>1
, then
V_{ij}
is an elementary T-singularity as in
[
68
, Proposition 3.10]
, also known as a Wahl singularity.
Using the change of variables
x=u^{n}
,
y=v^{n}
,
z=uv
, this singularity can be equivalently described as
{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(xy=z^{n})$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\frac{1}{n}(1,-1,a)$}\right.}
, and admits
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein smoothings by
[
68
, Definition 3.7]
.
More precisely, its universal
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein deformation space is irreducible, one-dimensional, and given by

{\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$(xy=z^{n}+t)$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\frac{1}{n}(1,-1,a,0)$}\right.}\,.

Taking
\{z=0\}
as deformation of
\partial V_{ij}
, we view this smoothing of
V_{ij}
as a one-parameter deformation of the pair
(V_{ij},\partial{V}_{ij})
.
If
n=1
, we consider the one-parameter deformation of
(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{u,v},\{uv=0\})
given by
(\mathbb{A}^{2}_{u,v},\{uv=t\})
, that is by just smoothing the normal crossing toric divisor.
Hence, for every value of
n
, we have a natural one-parameter
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein deformation of
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})
, whose general fiber is the germ of a smooth point with a smooth connected divisor.

Using the deformation theory results proved in §
4.1
, we will glue these local
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein deformations of the germs
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})
into a global deformation of
(\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})
.
Let
Q_{v}
be the number triangles
\Delta_{ij}
with
pr(p_{ij})=v
. As a projective toric variety
X
always satisfies
H^{1}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
, it follows from Lemma
4.2
that there exists a

Q_{v}
-dimensional

\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein deformation of

(\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial\widetilde{X}_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})

induced by the one-dimensional
\mathbb{Q}
-Gorenstein deformations of the
Q_{v}
germs
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})
. Moreover, the base of this
Q_{v}
-dimensional deformation is irreducible, since it is the case for each of the one-dimensional deformations of the germs
(V_{ij},\partial V_{ij})
.
We denote by
(X_{\mathscr{P}}^{v},\partial X_{\mathscr{P}}^{v})
a general fiber of this deformation. It is a log Calabi–Yau surface of charge
Q_{v}
, well-defined up to smooth deformations since the base of the deformation is irreducible.

STEP III:
In a third step we glue together the log Calabi-Yau surfaces
X_{\mathscr{P}}^{v}
for
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
obtained in Step II along the divisors corresponding to the edges of
\mathscr{P}
, to obtain the normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
. Note that such a gluing is not unique: if such an edge lies in the interior of
P
, the corresponding divisor is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, and if it is on the boundary we have an
\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Therefore, the set of all possible gluings of the surfaces
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
, for
v\in\mathscr{P}^{[0]}
, along a common divisor is a torsor for the torus
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{|\mathscr{P}^{1}|}
, where
|\mathscr{P}^{1}|
is the number of edges of the polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
on
P
. Each
\mathbb{C}^{\star}
acts by scaling of the gluing along the corresponding copy of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
or
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

Now that we obtained
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, we investigate its properties below. In particular, we show that it is a quasi-projective open Kulikov surface. To do that, we first describe the irreducible components
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
for
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
. The boundary
\partial P
of
P
is naturally identified with the intersection complex of the divisor
D
polarized by the restriction
L|_{D}
of
L
to
D
. As
D
is either a cycle of rational curves or a nodal curve of arithmetic genus one, it follows that
D
is topologically a circle, and is of integral length
\mathrm{deg}L|_{D}
.
Moreover, there are integral points
v_{x}\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
in one-to-one correspondence with the 0-dimensional strata
x
of
D
. If
x
and
y
are two 0-dimensional strata on the same irreducible component
D_{i}
of
D
, then there is a corresponding interval
I_{D_{i}}\subset\partial P\simeq S^{1}
with endpoints
v_{x}
and
v_{y}
, of integral length
\mathrm{deg}L|_{D_{i}}
, and containing no other vertex of the form
v_{z}
.

For every
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
, we denote by

(\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\partial\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}):=(\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}}),\mathrm{Spec}\,H^{0}(\partial X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathcal{O}_{\partial X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}}))

the affinization of
(X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\partial X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}})
.
In the following Lemmas
4.3
-
4.5
, we give an explicit description of the affine schemes
\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
and of the corresponding affinization map

X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
, which shows in particular that the algebras of regular functions

H^{0}(X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}})

and

H^{0}(\partial X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}},\mathcal{O}_{\partial X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}})

are finitely generated.
To do that, note that by Proposition
2.1
, for every 0-dimensional stratum
x
of
D
, the germ of
(Y,D)
at
x
is a cyclic quotient surface singularity, which can be described torically by a fan consisting of a single rational polyhedral cone
\sigma_{x}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface, with a polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
of a Symington polytope
P
defined as above.
For every
0
-dimensional stratum of
D
,
\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is an affine surface obtained by a flat deformation of the affine toric surface

\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
, and
\partial\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a flat locally trivial deformation of the union of the two toric divisors of
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
. Moreover, the affinization map

X^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a surjective projective birational morphism.

Proof.

Assume first that there is no interior vertex
p_{ij}
of a triangle
\Delta_{ij}
as in §
2.2
such that
pr(p_{ij})=v_{x}
. Then, by Step I of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2
,
X^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is the toric surface whose fan is given by the star of
\mathscr{P}
at
v_{x}
. Moreover, this fan is naturally a refinement of the strictly convex cone
\sigma_{x}
giving a local description of
P
near
v_{x}
. It follows that in this case the affinization
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is exactly the affine toric surface
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
, and the affinization map is a toric morphism induced by a refinement of fans, and so is surjective, projective, and birational.

In general, if there are points
p_{ij}
such that
pr(p_{ij})=v_{x}
, then, by Step II of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2
,
X^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a flat deformation, obtained by partial smoothing of the toric boundary, of the toric surface
X^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
whose fan is given by the star of
\mathscr{P}
at
v_{x}
, and whose affinization is given by
\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
. As
X^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a toric surface, we have
H^{1}(X^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}})=0
. Hence, by
[
96
, Theorem 1.4]
– see also
[
26
, Theorem 3.1]
, the affinization
X^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a flat deformation of the toric affinization
X^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}=\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
, and this implies
Lemma
4.3
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

The affine toric surface
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
is the dual quotient singularity of the quotient singularity
\mathrm{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}[\sigma_{x}^{\vee}\cap\mathbb{Z}^{2}]
describing the germ of
(Y,D)
at
x
.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface, with a polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
of a Symington polytope
P
defined as above.
For every vertex
v\in(\partial P)_{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
v\neq v_{x}
for all
0
-dimensional strata
x
of
D
, we have
\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and the affinization map

X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a surjective projective morphism with general fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

Proof.

Assume first that there is no interior vertex
p_{ij}
of a triangle
\Delta_{ij}
as in §
2.2
such that
pr(p_{ij})=v
. Then, by Step I of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2
,
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
is the toric surface whose fan is given by the star of
\mathscr{P}
at
v
.
Since
v\neq v_{x}
for all
0
-dimensional strata
x
of
D
, the boundary
\partial P
of
P
is straight at
v
, and so the fan of
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
contains two opposite rays coming from the two edges of
\partial P
containing
v
, and all the other rays are contained in a half-plane delimited by these two opposite rays. It follows that there exists a surjective projective toric morphism
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
with general fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In particular, the affinization
\overline{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
of
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
, and

X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the affinization morphism.

In general, if there are points
p_{ij}
such that
pr(p_{ij})=v
, then, by Step II of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2
,
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
is a flat deformation, obtained by partial smoothing of the toric boundary, of the toric surface
X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
whose fan is given by the star of
\mathscr{P}
at
v
. As in the previous case, we have a surjective projective toric morphism
X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
with general fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.
Since
X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
is a toric surface, we have
H^{1}(X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}},\mathcal{O}_{X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}})=0
, and so by
[
96
, Theorem 1.4]
, the morphism
X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}

deforms to a surjective projective morphism
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
with general fiber
\mathbb{P}^{1}
. This morphism is necessarily the affinization map of
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
, and so
Lemma
4.3
follows.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

When
Y
is smooth and the central point
p\in\overline{P}
is in the interior of
\overline{P}
, then we have
\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}
for every
0
-dimensional stratum
x
of
D
, and so
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is isomorphic to the
n
-vertex as in
[
41
, §1.3]
, where
n
is the number of irreducible components of
D
. In general, by Lemma
4.3
, the singularities of the irreducible components
\overline{X}^{v_{x}}_{\mathscr{P}}
at the central point
x_{0}
are flat deformations of cyclic quotient singularities, and so are also cyclic quotient singularities by
[
68
, Corollary 7.15]
.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface, with a polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
of a Symington polytope
P
defined as above.
Then, the normal crossing surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is a quasi-projective open Kulikov surface.

Proof.

It follows from the description of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in Lemmas
4.3
and
4.5
that
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is an open Kulikov surface. To prove quasi-projectivity, first recall that, by Definition
2.13
, the good polyhedral decomposition
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
is regular.
Hence, there exists a convex piecewise-linear function
\varphi
on
\overline{P}
inducing
\overline{\mathscr{P}}
. We denote by
L_{\varphi}
the corresponding ample line bundle on
\mathcal{X}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
.
It follows from the toric description of the deformation in Step I of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2

that the restrictions of
L_{\varphi}
to
\bigcup_{v^{\prime}\in pr^{-1}(v)}X^{v}_{\overline{\mathscr{P}}}
deform into ample line bundles
\widetilde{L}_{\varphi}^{v}
on the toric surfaces
\widetilde{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
As a toric surface
X
satisfies
H^{2}(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
, it follows from
deformation theory for line bundles (see for example the proof of
[
85
, Theorem 2.5.23 (ii)]
) that, in Step II of the construction of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
in §
4.2
,
the ample line bundles
L^{v}_{\varphi}
on
the toric surfaces
\widetilde{X}^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
deform
into ample line bundles
L^{v}_{\varphi}
on
the log Calabi–Yau surfaces
X^{v}_{\mathscr{P}}
.
Moreover, the line bundles
L^{v}_{\varphi}
and
L^{v^{\prime}}_{\varphi}

are isomorphic in restriction to
X^{e}_{\mathscr{P}}
, for every edge
e\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
adjacent to two vertices
v,v^{\prime}\in\mathscr{P}^{[1]}
.
Hence, it follows from the description of
\mathrm{Pic}(\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0})
in Lemma
3.10
that the ample line bundles
L^{v}_{\varphi}
glue into a global ample line bundle on
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
.
∎

4.3.
Semistable mirrors of polarized log Calabi–Yau surfaces

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a polarized log Calabi–Yau surface with a maximal degeneration defined by
(P,\mathscr{P})
.
By Theorem
3.21
, up to a locally trivial deformation, we can assume that the open Kulikov surface
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is
d
-semistable and generic. Therefore, by Theorem
3.26
, there exists a smoothing
\pi_{\mathscr{P}}:\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\to\Delta
of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, which in addition is an open Kulikov degeneration. From now on we refer to this smoothing as the
semistable mirror
to
(Y,D,L)
.

Remark 4.8
.

If
(Y,D)
is a toric pair, given by a toric surface
Y
with its anti-canonical boundary divisor
D
, then the affinization
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
of the semistable mirror is the completion along
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
of the three dimensional Gorenstein affine toric variety, whose fan is the cone over the momentum polytope
P
of
(Y,D,L)
and so which admits a natural toric morphism to
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
The semistable mirror

\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
is the completion along
0
of the toric crepant resolution of this affine toric singularity defined by the regular triangulation
\mathscr{P}
of
P
into triangles of size one.

Finally, note that while a smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is not unique, it is unique up to deformation. In the remaining part of this paper, we will always consider smoothings of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
up to deformation. Therefore by abuse of notation we will call
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}
“the” smoothing of
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P},0}
, and refer to it as the
semistable mirror
to the polarized log Calabi–Yau surface
(Y,D,L)
, with given Symington polytope
P
endowed with a good polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
.
In the following section §
5
, we show how to generalize the intrinsic mirror symmetry construction of Gross–Siebert for applying it to quasi-projective open Kulikov degenerations such as
\mathcal{X}_{\mathscr{P}}\rightarrow\Delta
.
Eventually, we show that starting with a polarized log Calabi–Yau
(Y,D,L)
, constructing its semistable mirror, and then taking the intrinsic mirror to the semistable mirror indeed leads to a family of polarized log Calabi–Yau’s deformation equivalent to the initial
(Y,D,L)
, as shown in §
8
. Therefore, the semistable mirror construction can be thought of as the reverse of a generalized intrinsic mirror construction of Gross–Siebert for families of log Calabi–Yau’s. Hence, the construction of the semistable mirror provides a way to talk of the “mirror of the mirror” of a polarized log Calabi–Yau
(Y,D,L)
with the choice of a Symington polytope
P
and a good polyhedral decomposition
\mathscr{P}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Let
(Y,D,L)
be a log Calabi–Yau surface with an ample line bundle
L
.
Since
L
is ample, we have
L\cdot\beta\in\mathbb{N}
for every effective curve class
\beta\in NE(Y)
. Hence, we have a map of monoids
NE[Y]\to\mathbb{N}
defined by
\beta\mapsto L\cdot\beta
, inducing a formal curve
\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{N}]\to\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[NE(Y)]
.
If
Y
smooth, the central fiber
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P},0}
is the
n
-vertex, obtained by gluing
\mathbb{A}^{2}
’s pairwise along
\mathbb{A}^{1}
’s as in
[
41
]
. In this case,
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathscr{P}}
is expected to be deformation equivalent to the restriction to
\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[\mathbb{N}]\to\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[NE(Y)]
of the mirror family
\overline{\mathcal{X}}_{GHK}\to\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbb{C}[NE(Y)]
to
(Y,D)
obtained by Gross–Hacking–Keel in
[
41
]
by smoothing the
n
-vertex.

5.
Mirror algebras for open Kulikov degenerations

Gross and Siebert developed an “Intrinsic Mirror Symmetry” program, which in particular explains how to construct an algebra of functions associated to degenerations of Calabi–Yau varieties
[
48
,
49
]
. In the following section, after shortly reviewing this construction, we explain how to adapt it to our situation, to construct an algebra of functions, referred to as the “mirror algebra”, for the mirror to an open Kulikov degeneration.

5.1.
Intrinsic mirror symmetry for projective degenerations

The intrinsic mirror symmetry construction particularly applies to a projective morphism
g\colon\mathcal{X}\to S
from a smooth variety
\mathcal{X}
with
K_{\mathcal{X}}=0
to a non-singular, non-complete curve
S
with single closed point
0\in S
, such that the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}=g^{-1}(0)
is a reduced simple normal crossing divisor on
\mathcal{X}
– this is a special situation of a so called “log smooth” morphism as in
[
48
, §1.1, pg 7]
. In particular, the schemes
\mathcal{X}
and
S
are viewed as “log schemes”, with a log structure defined by the data of the divisors
\mathcal{X}_{0}\subset\mathcal{X}
and
0\in S
respectively.

Associated to the total space
\mathcal{X}
is a cone complex, referred to as the tropicalization of
\mathcal{X}
and denoted by
\Sigma(\mathcal{X})
– see
[
1
, §2.1.4]
or
[
18
, §2.3.1]
. This cone complex can be viewed as the dual intersection complex of the pair
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{X}_{0})
, which contains a
k
-dimensional cone for each non-empty, connected intersection of
k
components of
\mathcal{X}_{0}
of codimension
k
. The mirror algebra for
g\colon\mathcal{X}\to S
constructed in
[
48
]
has a basis
\{\vartheta_{p}\}_{p\in\Sigma(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb{Z}}}
, indexed by the integral points of the tropicalization of
\mathcal{X}
, denoted by
\Sigma(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The mirror algebra depends on a choice of a monoid
P
, together with a monoid ideal
I
, defined as follows. Let
P\subset N_{1}(\mathcal{X}/S)
be a saturated and finitely generated monoid containing
NE(\mathcal{X}/S)
, where
N_{1}(\mathcal{X}/S)
is the group of relative curve classes, modulo numerical equivalence and
NE(\mathcal{X}/S)
is the monoid of integral points in the cone in
N_{1}(\mathcal{X}/S)\otimes\mathbb{R}
generated by effective curve classes. Let
I\subset P
be a monoid ideal such that
P\setminus I
is finite, that is, such that
S_{I}(\mathcal{X}):=\mathbb{C}[P]/I
is an Artinian algebra.
Then, the mirror algebra is an
S_{I}(\mathcal{X})
-algebra structure on the free
S_{I}(\mathcal{X})
-module

R_{I}(\mathcal{X})=\bigoplus_{p\in\Sigma(X)_{\mathbb{Z}}}S_{I}(\mathcal{X})\vartheta_{p}\,.

For every
p,q\in\Sigma(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb{Z}}
, the product is given by

(5.1)

\vartheta_{p}\cdot\vartheta_{q}=\sum_{r\in\Sigma(\mathcal{X})_{\mathbb{Z}}}\sum_{\beta\in P}N_{pqr}^{\beta}(\mathcal{X})t^{\beta}\vartheta_{r}

as explained in
[
48
]
, where
N_{pqr}^{\beta}(\mathcal{X})
is a punctured Gromov–Witten invariant of
\mathcal{X}
, enumerating punctured log maps, that are given by rational curves of class
\beta
with three marked points
x_{p},x_{q},x_{r}
with contact orders
p,q,-r
with
\mathcal{X}_{0}
. In addition, the image of
x_{r}
is required to be a given point in the interior of the stratum
Z_{r}
of
\mathcal{X}
corresponding to the smallest cone of
\Sigma(\mathcal{X})
containing
r
. Note that such curves, with negative order of tangency
-r\neq 0
live in the log scheme
\mathcal{X}_{0}
, where the log structure is obtained by restricting the log structure on
\mathcal{X}
.

5.2.
Mirror algebras

We cannot directly apply the intrinsic mirror symmetry construction of
[
48
]
to an open Kulikov degeneration
\pi\colon\mathcal{X}\to\Delta
for three reasons. First,
\mathcal{X}
is not an algebraic variety over an algebraic curve, but only a formal scheme over the formal disk
\Delta
. Second, the map
\pi:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\Delta
is not projective. Third, the central fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
is a normal crossing but possibly not simple normal crossing. We will address these issues to define punctured Gromov–Witten invariants
N_{pqr}^{\beta}(\mathcal{X})
for
\mathcal{X}
. The first issue will be addressed by reformulating the constructions of
[
48
]
using only the special fiber
\mathcal{X}_{0}
